# SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES: STANDARDS AND TESTS

**Last completed October Term:** 1996
**Processed through:** Complete rendered October Term 1996, including all supplied events and admitted Immediate Judicial Consequences through June 26, 1998.
**Edition:** August 18, 2026

## Article III and Federal Courts

### Specialized statutory review-channel preclusion

**Governing question:** When a comprehensive federal administrative-review scheme displaces initial district-court jurisdiction over a claim connected to that scheme.

**Current rule:** A specialized statutory scheme precludes initial district-court review when congressional intent to channel the dispute is fairly discernible from the enacted structure and the designated route preserves meaningful judicial review. A closely connected claim ordinarily must use that route.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Examine the enacted review structure first, then determine whether the asserted claim can receive meaningful consideration through the designated administrative and appellate sequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** Initial district-court review remains available when the statutory route cannot provide meaningful judicial review, the claim is genuinely collateral to the scheme, or the process imposes an irreparable deprivation the designated review cannot address.

**First controlling authority:** Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich, 510 U.S. 200 (1994), decided January 19, 1994, Statutory review-channel preclusion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich, 510 U.S. 200 (1994), decided January 19, 1994, Statutory review-channel preclusion; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich, 510 U.S. 200 (1994), January 19, 1994.

### Federal constitutional claims under the FTCA and agency waivers

**Governing question:** Whether a federal constitutional claim falls within the Federal Tort Claims Act and whether a federal agency’s sue-and-be-sued clause removes immunity.

**Current rule:** A federal constitutional claim is not cognizable under 28 U.S.C. § 1346(b) because it arises under federal rather than state law and lacks the required private-person state-law analogue. The FTCA therefore does not make the United States the exclusive defendant on that claim. A broad agency sue-and-be-sued clause may waive the agency’s sovereign immunity, but the waiver does not itself create a substantive cause of action.

**Controlling construction:** Sections 1346(b) and 2679(a) do not convert a federal constitutional claim into an FTCA claim or make the FTCA exclusive when § 1346(b) is not satisfied.

**Exceptions and limits:** A separate cause of action and authorized remedy remain necessary even when immunity is waived.

**First controlling authority:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, FTCA exclusivity and the sue-and-be-sued waiver; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, FTCA exclusivity and the sue-and-be-sued waiver; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), February 23, 1994.

### Constitutional damages actions against federal agencies

**Governing question:** Whether the officer damages remedy recognized in Bivens extends directly to a federal agency.

**Current rule:** Bivens does not imply a damages cause of action directly against a federal agency, even when the agency’s sovereign immunity has been waived. The judicially implied remedy remains directed to responsible federal officers rather than the employing agency.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** A constitutional damages claim brought directly against the federal agency must be dismissed absent an independent statutory cause of action.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not narrow existing Bivens actions against individual officers or foreclose an otherwise authorized statutory or prospective remedy.

**First controlling authority:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, Bivens liability of a federal agency; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, Bivens liability of a federal agency; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** FDIC v. Meyer, 510 U.S. 471 (1994), February 23, 1994.

### Qualified-immunity review uses the complete body of law

**Governing question:** What legal authorities an appellate court must consider when deciding whether a right was clearly established.

**Current rule:** Because clearly established law is an objective legal question reviewed de novo, a court must consider all materially relevant precedent available at the time of the challenged conduct, including authority not cited to the district court. A party’s incomplete legal research does not bind the court to an incomplete statement of governing law.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not excuse failure to preserve the claim itself or the factual premises on which it depends.

**First controlling authority:** Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, Legal authorities considered on qualified-immunity review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510 (1994), decided February 23, 1994, Legal authorities considered on qualified-immunity review; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510 (1994), February 23, 1994.

### Ancillary jurisdiction to enforce settlements after dismissal

**Governing question:** When a federal court may enforce a settlement after a Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) dismissal.

**Current rule:** A federal district court lacks ancillary jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after a Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) dismissal when the dismissal neither incorporates the settlement terms nor expressly retains enforcement jurisdiction. Enforcement remains ancillary when the federal dismissal makes compliance part of the decree or expressly retains jurisdiction; otherwise an independent jurisdictional basis is required.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** Without incorporation, retained jurisdiction, or an independent federal jurisdictional ground, the enforcement proceeding must be dismissed.

**Exceptions and limits:** The parties may pursue an independent state contract action or a separate federal action supported by diversity or another jurisdictional grant.

**First controlling authority:** Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 511 U.S. 375 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Ancillary jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after dismissal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 511 U.S. 375 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Ancillary jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after dismissal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 511 U.S. 375 (1994), May 16, 1994.

### Collateral-order review of settlement-enforcement rulings

**Governing question:** Whether an order vacating a settlement-based dismissal or refusing to enforce a settlement is immediately appealable.

**Current rule:** An order vacating a settlement-based dismissal or refusing to enforce a settlement is not immediately appealable under the collateral-order doctrine. The private contractual interest in avoiding continued litigation lacks the exceptional public and legal importance required to overcome the final-judgment rule, and the ruling can be reviewed after final judgment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern constitutional or statutory immunities whose value would be destroyed by trial. Section 1292(b), final review, and extraordinary mandamus remain available when their independent requirements are met.

**First controlling authority:** Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc., 511 U.S. 863 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Collateral-order appeal from refusal to enforce a settlement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc., 511 U.S. 863 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Collateral-order appeal from refusal to enforce a settlement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc., 511 U.S. 863 (1994), June 6, 1994.

### Habeas channeling of conviction-invalidating § 1983 damages claims

**Governing question:** When a claimant in custody may pursue § 1983 relief on a theory that would necessarily invalidate an outstanding conviction, sentence, or disciplinary credit judgment.

**Current rule:** While a claimant remains in custody and habeas is legally available, a § 1983 damages or declaratory claim whose success would necessarily establish the invalidity of an outstanding conviction, sentence, or prison disciplinary judgment affecting custody duration may not proceed. The consequence of the claim controls, not whether it is labeled procedural. A genuinely separable claim, including prospective relief governing future procedure, may proceed when success would not imply invalidity of the outstanding judgment.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** The invalidity-implying claim is dismissed without prejudice rather than stayed merely to require exhaustion. A stay remains possible for a different independently lawful reason.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar claims concerning conditions unrelated to custody duration, every procedural challenge to discipline, or prospective relief whose legal premise does not invalidate the earlier judgment. The treatment of a claimant for whom habeas is unavailable through no fault remains open.

**First controlling authority:** Heck v. Humphrey, 512 U.S. 477 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Coordination of § 1983 damages with an outstanding conviction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Heck v. Humphrey, 512 U.S. 477 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, supplies the conviction-and-sentence rule. Edwards v. Balisok, 520 U.S. 641 (1997), decided May 19, 1997, extends the rule to disciplinary good-time judgments, preserves separable prospective relief, and requires dismissal without prejudice.

**Present operation effective:** Edwards v. Balisok, 520 U.S. 641 (1997), May 19, 1997.

**Development:**
- May 19, 1997, Edwards v. Balisok, 520 U.S. 641 (1997): Heck is extended to disciplinary judgments revoking good-time credits when success would necessarily imply invalidity; separable prospective relief remains available, and the unavailable claim is dismissed without prejudice.

### Capital habeas counsel and prepetition stay authority

**Governing question:** When a capital federal postconviction proceeding begins for appointment of counsel and related stay authority.

**Current rule:** A capital prisoner’s motion requesting appointment of federal habeas counsel commences the represented postconviction proceeding for purposes of 21 U.S.C. § 848(q)(4)(B). Once that right is invoked, the same proceeding is pending for purposes of 28 U.S.C. § 2251, and the district court may stay execution in its sound discretion when reasonably necessary to make the representation effective.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The court appoints qualified counsel, then determines whether and for how long a stay is reasonably necessary to permit investigation, preparation, and filing of a meaningful first federal petition.

**Exceptions and limits:** A stay is not automatic or indefinite. Inexcusable delay or disregard of available procedures may justify denial, and the decision excuses no later procedural default.

**First controlling authority:** McFarland v. Scott, 512 U.S. 849 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Commencement of the represented capital proceeding; established the commencement rule; the companion stay component is supplied by the same decision’s stay-authority holding.

**Controlling authorities:** McFarland v. Scott, 512 U.S. 849 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Commencement of the represented capital proceeding; supplies the commencement rule; the companion stay component is supplied by the same decision’s stay-authority holding.

**Present operation effective:** McFarland v. Scott, 512 U.S. 849 (1994), June 30, 1994.

### FEC authority to seek certiorari and untimely Solicitor General authorization

**Governing question:** Whether the FEC may independently petition for certiorari and whether untimely Solicitor General authorization can validate the petition.

**Current rule:** Section 437d(a)(6) does not independently authorize the FEC to petition this Court for certiorari, and the Solicitor General’s authorization after expiration of § 2101(c)’s filing period cannot relate back to validate the petition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether the nonvoting congressional officers violated separation of powers, whether the ex officio provision was severable, or whether later Commission ratification could cure a structural defect.

**First controlling authority:** Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, 513 U.S. 88, decided December 6, 1994, FEC authority to petition for certiorari and untimely authorization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, 513 U.S. 88, decided December 6, 1994, FEC authority to petition for certiorari and untimely authorization; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, 513 U.S. 88, December 6, 1994.

### Jurisdictional characterization

**Governing question:** Whether the ministerial protection defeats federal-question jurisdiction or instead operates as a merits defense to applying Title VII.

**Current rule:** The ministerial protection is a constitutional limitation on applying Title VII to the merits, not a withdrawal of federal-question jurisdiction. A federal court with statutory jurisdiction over a substantial federal employment claim resolves the church-autonomy defense on the merits. It does not dismiss under Rule 12(b)(1) merely because the Constitution forecloses application of the statute.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not address a federal claim that is immaterial, wholly insubstantial, or pleaded solely to manufacture jurisdiction.

**First controlling authority:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, decided January 17, 1995, Jurisdictional characterization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, decided January 17, 1995, Jurisdictional characterization; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, January 17, 1995.

### Preservation of the governmental-entity argument

**Governing question:** When a party may raise a new legal argument supporting a preserved claim without presenting a new claim or form of relief.

**Current rule:** A party may present a new legal argument supporting a preserved claim when the argument was passed upon below, falls within the question presented, and seeks no new claim or form of relief.

**First controlling authority:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, decided February 21, 1995, Preservation of the governmental-entity argument; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, decided February 21, 1995, Preservation of the governmental-entity argument; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, February 21, 1995.

### Federal review when state and federal grounds are interwoven

**Governing question:** When the Supreme Court may review a state judgment whose state and federal grounds are interwoven.

**Current rule:** The Supreme Court may review a state judgment that fairly appears to rest on federal law or on state law interwoven with the federal question when no adequate and independent state ground is clearly stated.

**First controlling authority:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Federal jurisdiction over an interwoven state-law judgment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Federal jurisdiction over an interwoven state-law judgment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, March 1, 1995.

### Interlocutory review of municipal liability

**Governing question:** Whether an interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal permits review of a separate nonappealable municipal-liability ruling.

**Current rule:** A court of appeals reviewing an interlocutory denial of qualified immunity to individual officers may not also review a nonappealable municipal-liability ruling merely because the matters arise from the same litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Section 1292(b), mandamus, and any genuinely inseparable issue necessary to meaningful review remain governed by their own requirements. The Court does not create general pendent party appellate jurisdiction.

**First controlling authority:** Swint v. Chambers County Commission, 514 U.S. 35, decided March 1, 1995, Interlocutory review of municipal liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Swint v. Chambers County Commission, 514 U.S. 35, decided March 1, 1995, Interlocutory review of municipal liability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Swint v. Chambers County Commission, 514 U.S. 35, March 1, 1995.

### Obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review

**Governing question:** Whether a party may disregard a bankruptcy injunction before obtaining a stay, modification, or reversal through authorized review.

**Current rule:** Parties subject to a bankruptcy injunction issued by a court with at least a colorable basis for jurisdiction must obey the order until it is stayed, modified, or reversed through authorized review. They may not obtain collateral permission from another court to act in defiance of it. The duty to comply is distinct from the ultimate validity of the injunction. A party with notice must challenge the order in the issuing court and through the prescribed appellate route unless the order is transparently beyond any arguable jurisdictional basis.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not approve every injunction protecting a nondebtor surety, decide the ultimate permissible breadth of relief under § 105, or require obedience to an order entered without even an arguable jurisdictional foundation.

**First controlling authority:** Celotex Corp. v. Edwards, 514 U.S. 300, decided April 19, 1995, Obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Celotex Corp. v. Edwards, 514 U.S. 300, decided April 19, 1995, Obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Celotex Corp. v. Edwards, 514 U.S. 300, April 19, 1995.

### Refund jurisdiction for a compelled third-party payment

**Governing question:** Whether a person who was not the assessed taxpayer may use § 1346(a)(1) after paying under protest to remove a federal tax lien from the person’s own property.

**Current rule:** Section 1346(a)(1) authorizes a refund action by a person who was not the assessed taxpayer but paid the tax under protest to remove a federal tax lien from her own property. A claimant from whom the Government compelled payment through a tax lien on the claimant’s property may invoke § 1346(a)(1), provided the claimant satisfies the applicable administrative and procedural requirements. The waiver is applied through its enacted reference to a tax erroneously or illegally collected, not limited categorically to the person named in the assessment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not determine when a volunteer or stranger who pays another person’s tax may sue. It is confined to a compelled payment tied to a concrete lien against the claimant’s property.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Williams, 514 U.S. 527, decided April 25, 1995, Refund jurisdiction for a compelled third-party payment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Williams, 514 U.S. 527, decided April 25, 1995, Refund jurisdiction for a compelled third-party payment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Williams, 514 U.S. 527, April 25, 1995.

### Evidence-sufficiency disputes in qualified-immunity appeals

**Governing question:** Which issues are immediately appealable when a qualified-immunity denial rests on evidence sufficiency.

**Current rule:** A denial of qualified immunity is not immediately appealable insofar as it determines only that the pretrial record contains sufficient evidence to create a genuine factual dispute. An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may present the legal question whether conduct, taken under an assumed or properly supported version of the facts, violated clearly established law. It does not authorize appellate reconsideration of whose account the evidence supports.

**Exceptions and limits:** Immediate review remains available for a separable legal immunity question. Section 1292(b), mandamus, and final appellate review remain governed by their own requirements.

**First controlling authority:** Johnson v. Jones, 515 U.S. 304, decided June 12, 1995, Evidence-sufficiency disputes in qualified-immunity appeals; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Johnson v. Jones, 515 U.S. 304, decided June 12, 1995, Evidence-sufficiency disputes in qualified-immunity appeals; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Johnson v. Jones, 515 U.S. 304, June 12, 1995.

### Judicial review of Westfall Act certification

**Governing question:** Whether and at what stage a court may review the Attorney General’s Westfall Act scope-of-employment certification.

**Current rule:** An Attorney General certification that a federal employee acted within the scope of employment is judicially reviewable before substitution of the United States becomes final. Certification initiates substitution and is conclusive for removal where the statute expressly says so. It does not conclusively determine the scope-of-employment question for final substitution. The district court may receive evidence and resolve disputed facts under ordinary procedures.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not determine whether Lamagno acted within the scope of employment, waive federal immunity, create a cause of action against the United States, or decide the federal jurisdiction that would remain after a court rejects certification in a case lacking another jurisdictional basis.

**First controlling authority:** Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno, 515 U.S. 417, decided June 14, 1995, Judicial review of Westfall Act certification; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno, 515 U.S. 417, decided June 14, 1995, Judicial review of Westfall Act certification; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno, 515 U.S. 417, June 14, 1995.

### Standing for racial-gerrymandering claims

**Governing question:** When a voter has standing to challenge a district as an unconstitutional racial classification.

**Current rule:** Voters who reside outside the district alleged to embody an unconstitutional racial classification lack standing absent evidence that the legislature personally classified or otherwise concretely injured them.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether District 4 is unconstitutional, whether residents of that district would have standing, or what equal-protection standard would govern a properly presented claim.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Hays / Louisiana v. Hays, 515 U.S. 737, decided June 29, 1995, Standing for voters outside the challenged district; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Hays / Louisiana v. Hays, 515 U.S. 737, decided June 29, 1995, Standing for voters outside the challenged district; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Hays / Louisiana v. Hays, 515 U.S. 737, June 29, 1995.

### Ancillary enforcement jurisdiction and new-defendant liability

**Governing question:** Whether ancillary enforcement jurisdiction permits a new action imposing liability for an existing judgment on a nonparty through new facts or legal theories.

**Current rule:** A federal court’s ancillary enforcement jurisdiction does not support a new action imposing personal liability for an existing judgment on a nonparty through new facts and legal theories. Such an action requires an independent statutory basis of federal jurisdiction.

**Exceptions and limits:** Whether Peacock diverted assets, whether state law permits recovery, whether diversity jurisdiction exists, and whether any separate federal cause of action reaches the alleged conduct.

**First controlling authority:** Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349, decided February 21, 1996, Ancillary enforcement jurisdiction and new-defendant liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349, decided February 21, 1996, Ancillary enforcement jurisdiction and new-defendant liability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349, February 21, 1996.

**Development:**
- February 21, 1996, Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349: *Kokkonen* is clarified beyond settlement enforcement. Ancillary jurisdiction may implement an existing federal decree, but it does not create adjudicatory power over a new defendant and a new liability controversy. The decision does not limit garnishment, receivership, contempt, or other process directed to property legally belonging to the original debtor.

### Successive collateral-order appeal of qualified immunity

**Governing question:** When a later denial of qualified immunity creates a new collateral-order appeal and what that appeal may review.

**Current rule:** Each order denying qualified immunity is assessed according to what that order decides. A later order is immediately appealable to the extent it determines whether conduct, taken under the facts assumed by the district court or the plaintiff-supported version of disputed facts, violates clearly established law. Immediate review does not extend to whether the record is sufficient to prove those facts.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize repeated appeals from unchanged orders, review of discovery management, reconsideration of witness credibility, or repackaging of an evidence-sufficiency argument as a legal issue.

**First controlling authority:** Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299, decided February 21, 1996, Successive collateral-order appeal of qualified immunity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299, decided February 21, 1996, Successive collateral-order appeal of qualified immunity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299, February 21, 1996.

**Development:**
- February 21, 1996, Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299: *Johnson v. Jones* is applied and clarified. Its factual-sufficiency bar remains unchanged, while the present decision establishes that a materially later order may generate a new appealable legal immunity question. *Swint* remains unaffected.

### No implied repeal by exclusive Exchange Act jurisdiction

**Governing question:** Whether the Exchange Act’s exclusive federal-jurisdiction provision impliedly displaces § 1738 for a state settlement judgment releasing Exchange Act claims.

**Current rule:** The Exchange Act’s exclusive federal-jurisdiction provision does not impliedly repeal § 1738 for a state judgment approving the release of Exchange Act claims.

**First controlling authority:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, No implied repeal by exclusive Exchange Act jurisdiction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, No implied repeal by exclusive Exchange Act jurisdiction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, February 27, 1996.

### Scope of an accepted § 1292(b) appeal

**Governing question:** Which issues a court of appeals may decide after accepting a § 1292(b) appeal from a certified order.

**Current rule:** Section 1292(b) authorizes an appeal from the certified order. The court of appeals may resolve an antecedent or necessary issue contained within that order, but may not use the appeal to review a separate order or unrelated matter lacking its own appellate basis.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize plenary interlocutory review of every issue in the litigation.

**First controlling authority:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, Scope of an accepted § 1292(b) appeal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, Scope of an accepted § 1292(b) appeal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, January 9, 1996.

**Development:**
- January 9, 1996, Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199: An accepted § 1292(b) appeal reaches antecedent or necessary issues within the certified order, but it does not create appellate authority over a separate order or unrelated matter.

### Appealability of an abstention remand

**Governing question:** When an abstention remand is immediately appealable despite § 1447(d).

**Current rule:** Section 1447(d) bars review only of remands authorized by § 1447(c). An abstention remand that conclusively surrenders the federal action to a state court, determines a question separate from the merits, and cannot later be reviewed in the federal case is immediately appealable.

**First controlling authority:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, decided June 3, 1996, Appealability of an abstention remand; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, decided June 3, 1996, Appealability of an abstention remand; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, June 3, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 3, 1996, Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706: *Digital Equipment* is clarified rather than enlarged generally. Immediate appeal rests on the special finality of an abstention remand that conclusively surrenders the entire federal action to a state court.

### Burford abstention in a damages action

**Governing question:** What relief a federal court may use when Burford abstention applies to an action seeking legal damages.

**Current rule:** A federal court may decline equitable or otherwise discretionary relief in an appropriate abstention case. When legal damages are sought within existing jurisdiction, the court may postpone adjudication through a necessary and proportionate stay while a material state-law issue is resolved, but it ordinarily must retain the action.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not prevent remand on an independently valid statutory ground, require a stay, or decide arbitrability.

**First controlling authority:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, decided June 3, 1996, Burford abstention in a damages action; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, decided June 3, 1996, Burford abstention in a damages action; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., 517 U.S. 706, June 3, 1996.

### Removal defect cured before trial

**Governing question:** Whether an erroneous refusal to remand destroys a later diversity judgment after complete diversity lawfully arises before trial.

**Current rule:** A district court must remand while a removal defect remains. An earlier erroneous refusal to remand does not invalidate a completed diversity judgment when complete diversity arises lawfully and noncollusively before trial and continues through final judgment.

**Exceptions and limits:** Finality cannot cure absent subject-matter jurisdiction, collusive manufacture of diversity, or improper manipulation. Independently authorized fees or sanctions for wrongful removal remain separate.

**First controlling authority:** Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis, 519 U.S. 61 (1996), decided December 10, 1996, Removal defect cured before trial; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis, 519 U.S. 61 (1996), decided December 10, 1996, Removal defect cured before trial; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis, 519 U.S. 61 (1996), December 10, 1996.

### Pre-enforcement injury from a directly prohibited existing business

**Governing question:** When a regulated business has standing and a ripe controversy to challenge a newly enacted criminal prohibition before enforcement.

**Current rule:** A business presents a ripe Article III controversy when the enactment directly prohibits its existing operation, causes immediate cessation, threatens serious criminal consequences for resumption, and government has not disavowed enforcement.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary standing and ripeness remain unsatisfied when planned conduct is uncertain, materially different from the prohibition, or subject only to remote enforcement.

**First controlling authority:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Pre-enforcement justiciability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Pre-enforcement justiciability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Actual injury for prisoner access-to-courts claims

**Governing question:** What injury a prisoner must show to establish denial of access to the courts.

**Current rule:** The claimant must identify the nonfrivolous legal channel affected and a present official barrier that causes or imminently threatens material impairment. A prisoner need not await final dismissal when the barrier is already preventing timely filing or meaningful presentation. Abstract inadequacy, inconvenience, or preference for a better program is insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not require proof of ultimate success on the underlying claim, guarantee optimal legal research, or extend protected access beyond the identified legal channels.

**First controlling authority:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Actual injury and scope of protected access; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Actual injury and scope of protected access; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, June 24, 1996.

## Due Process

### Pre-seizure process for civil forfeiture of real property

**Governing question:** What process is required before the Government seizes real property for civil forfeiture.

**Current rule:** Except upon particularized proof of exigent circumstances, the Fifth Amendment requires notice and a meaningful opportunity to be heard before the Government seizes real property for civil forfeiture.

**Burdens and proof:** The Government bears the obligation to establish an actual exigency justifying immediate seizure.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule concerns real property and permits immediate action on proof of a genuine exigency. It does not decide the treatment of readily movable personal property or contraband.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Pre-seizure process for real property; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Pre-seizure process for real property; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), December 13, 1993.

### Specific constitutional source and substantive-due-process nonduplication

**Governing question:** Whether substantive due process supplies a parallel claim when a specific constitutional provision directly governs the alleged arrest or personal restraint.

**Current rule:** A claimant may not use substantive due process to duplicate Fourth Amendment protection for injuries alleged to arise from arrest and prosecution-related restraints on the person. When settled Fourth Amendment law supplies the directly relevant source, substantive due process does not create a parallel federal malicious-prosecution tort.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule leaves open a materially distinct nonseizure injury and does not decide a properly pleaded Fourth Amendment, fabricated-evidence, procedural-due-process, or state-law claim.

**First controlling authority:** Albright v. Oliver, 510 U.S. 266 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Substantive due process as the asserted source; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Albright v. Oliver, 510 U.S. 266 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Substantive due process as the asserted source; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Albright v. Oliver, 510 U.S. 266 (1994), January 24, 1994.

### Whole-charge review of reasonable-doubt instructions

**Governing question:** How a reviewing court determines whether a reasonable-doubt instruction lowered the prosecution’s burden.

**Current rule:** The Constitution prescribes no fixed wording for a reasonable-doubt instruction. A reviewing court asks whether there is a reasonable likelihood that the jury understood the complete charge to permit conviction on proof below reasonable doubt, evaluating disputed phrases in the context of the directions concerning evidence, the prosecution’s burden, and the required abiding conviction of guilt.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not endorse archaic moral-certainty language; similar wording may be unconstitutional when context fails to anchor the required certainty in the evidence or combines phrases that materially lower the burden.

**First controlling authority:** Victor v. Nebraska / Sandoval v. California, 511 U.S. 1 (1994), decided March 22, 1994, Constitutional standard for reasonable-doubt instructions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Victor v. Nebraska / Sandoval v. California, 511 U.S. 1 (1994), decided March 22, 1994, Constitutional standard for reasonable-doubt instructions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Victor v. Nebraska / Sandoval v. California, 511 U.S. 1 (1994), March 22, 1994.

### Burden for proving incompetence to stand trial

**Governing question:** What burden a State may place on a criminal defendant who asserts incompetence to stand trial.

**Current rule:** Due process forbids requiring a criminal defendant to prove incompetence by clear and convincing evidence. A State may presume competence and place a preponderance burden on the defendant, but its procedure may not permit trial after the defendant has shown that incompetence is more probable than competence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not require the prosecution to prove competence beyond a reasonable doubt, prohibit a rebuttable presumption of competence, decide a defendant’s actual competence, or establish standards for civil commitment.

**First controlling authority:** Cooper v. Oklahoma, 517 U.S. 348, decided April 16, 1996, Burden for proving incompetence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Cooper v. Oklahoma, 517 U.S. 348, decided April 16, 1996, Burden for proving incompetence; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Cooper v. Oklahoma, 517 U.S. 348, April 16, 1996.

### Meaningful judicial review of punitive-damages amounts

**Governing question:** What postverdict procedure a State must provide when it authorizes punitive damages.

**Current rule:** A State that authorizes punitive damages must provide meaningful judicial review of the amount, including review of whether the award rests on legally permissible punitive considerations and remains within the range a rational jury could derive from the evidence. Review need not be de novo or use a fixed verbal formula, but it must be capable of correcting an amount outside the rational range supported by the record.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no numerical ratio, proof burden, or mandatory remittitur formula and leaves the State discretion to select a constitutionally adequate corrective procedure.

**First controlling authority:** Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg, 512 U.S. 415 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Procedural safeguard against arbitrary punitive amounts; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg, 512 U.S. 415 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Procedural safeguard against arbitrary punitive amounts; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg, 512 U.S. 415 (1994), June 24, 1994.

### Withdrawal of an apparent postdeprivation tax remedy

**Governing question:** Whether a State may withdraw a previously available postdeprivation tax-refund remedy after taxpayers paid in reliance on it.

**Current rule:** When a State holds out a clear postdeprivation refund remedy for unlawfully collected taxes, due process bars it from withdrawing that remedy after taxpayers have paid under the apparent remedial scheme. A State may choose an adequate predeprivation system, an adequate postdeprivation system, or a combination. It may change that system prospectively with fair notice. It may not require payment under an apparently available refund route and then reinterpret that route away after payment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not dictate the design of Georgia’s future tax procedures. The State may adopt prospective protest, prepayment, limitations, and proof requirements that provide constitutionally adequate notice and opportunity.

**First controlling authority:** Reich v. Collins, 513 U.S. 106, decided December 6, 1994, Withdrawal of an apparent postdeprivation tax remedy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reich v. Collins, 513 U.S. 106, decided December 6, 1994, Withdrawal of an apparent postdeprivation tax remedy; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reich v. Collins, 513 U.S. 106, December 6, 1994.

### Facial review of previability abortion regulations

**Governing question:** What standard and denominator govern a facial challenge to a previability abortion regulation.

**Current rule:** *Casey*, not *Salerno*’s no-set-of-circumstances formulation, governs a facial challenge to a regulation of previability abortion. A provision is invalid when, in a large fraction of the cases in which it operates as a restriction, its purpose or predictable effect is to place a substantial obstacle in the path of a person seeking a previability abortion. Persons for whom the provision is irrelevant do not enlarge the denominator.

**First controlling authority:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Facial review under Casey; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Facial review under Casey; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, February 13, 1995.

### Operational and cumulative undue-burden review

**Governing question:** How courts attribute and aggregate burdens when linked abortion requirements operate against existing access conditions.

**Current rule:** Courts must measure the additional burden attributable to the challenged provision as it operates against actual access conditions, and must consider linked requirements together when their combined operation determines whether a substantial obstacle exists. Geographic distance, provider availability, scheduling limitations, cost, and comparable conditions are not automatically attributable to the State. They remain relevant to identifying what the enactment adds to the claimant’s circumstances. Requirements may not be insulated from review through artificial isolation when they operate as an integrated restriction.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not hold that a twenty-four-hour period is always valid or always invalid. Nor does cumulative analysis justify relief against a provision that contributes nothing to the combined constitutional burden.

**First controlling authority:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Operational burden and the remand record; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Operational burden and the remand record; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, February 13, 1995.

### Usable medical-emergency routes in penal abortion regulation

**Governing question:** What emergency route and notice a penal abortion regulation must provide to permit prompt medical action.

**Current rule:** A penal abortion regulation must preserve a practically usable emergency route permitting prompt action in the physician’s good-faith clinical judgment when delay creates a material risk of death, serious physical injury, substantial irreversible impairment, or comparable grave medically established harm. A court may adopt a saving construction only when the enacted text and controlling state law fairly bear it. Applicable state scienter rules must be included in the notice analysis.

**Exceptions and limits:** The emergency route does not immunize negligent practice or an unsupported invocation of medical necessity.

**First controlling authority:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Medical emergency, statutory definitions, and penal notice; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Medical emergency, statutory definitions, and penal notice; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, February 13, 1995.

### State-created liberty in prison discipline

**Governing question:** When state prison law creates a protected liberty interest in avoiding disciplinary segregation.

**Current rule:** A State creates a protected prison liberty interest when substantive law meaningfully limits officials’ authority to impose a significant deprivation. Mandatory wording alone is insufficient, but a court may not disregard a genuine legal constraint on punitive segregation. The court examines both the substance of state law and the deprivation at issue. State law must genuinely constrain official authority rather than merely prescribe procedures or use mandatory drafting. The restraint must concern a meaningful loss of liberty rather than a routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or ordinary discretionary condition of confinement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not constitutionalize every prison regulation, routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or discretionary management choice. Institutional security and lawful correctional judgment retain their ordinary weight.

**First controlling authority:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, decided June 19, 1995, State-created liberty in prison discipline; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, decided June 19, 1995, State-created liberty in prison discipline; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, June 19, 1995.

### Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims

**Governing question:** When a federal court must give full faith and credit to a state settlement judgment releasing exclusively federal claims.

**Current rule:** The federal court first determines the effect the rendering State gives its judgment. It then applies that effect unless Congress expressly or by irreconcilable implication displaced § 1738, or the judgment is not entitled to recognition because the rendering court lacked jurisdiction over the action before it or the judgment failed constitutional due process.

**First controlling authority:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, February 27, 1996.

**Development:**
- February 27, 1996, Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for state class judgments releasing exclusively federal claims. Section 1738 remains the starting point; exclusive federal jurisdiction alone creates no exception. A constitutionally adequate class process remains a prerequisite to binding absent members.

### Parole information when parole remains legally possible

**Governing question:** Whether Simmons requires parole information when parole remains legally possible but release is practically unlikely.

**Current rule:** *Simmons* requires correction of a legally false release premise when the only noncapital sentence forecloses parole. It does not require courts to predict future parole-board decisions or convert practical estimates of release into constitutionally fixed sentencing facts.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State remains free to provide accurate, nonmisleading information about a legally fixed minimum period of confinement. The holding does not narrow *Simmons* when the life sentence legally eliminates parole.

**First controlling authority:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Parole information when parole remains legally possible; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Parole information when parole remains legally possible; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213: *Simmons* is declined extension beyond legal parole ineligibility; practical estimates of release do not become constitutionally fixed sentencing facts while parole remains legally possible.

### Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion

**Governing question:** What representation and process are required before a judgment may bind a person who was not a party.

**Current rule:** Shared taxpayer status, a common public issue, and similar litigation interests do not themselves create representation. Binding representation requires a recognized legal relationship, genuinely aligned interests, responsibility to protect the absent person, and procedure adequate to make that responsibility effective. Notice is ordinarily required when practicable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision leaves intact ordinary privity, actual control of prior litigation, adequately constituted class actions, fiduciary representation, and suits brought by legally authorized public representatives.

**First controlling authority:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, decided June 10, 1996, Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, decided June 10, 1996, Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793: *Matsushita* is applied and clarified. A judgment may bind absent persons only when the rendering process satisfies the constitutional prerequisites of notice and adequate, legally accountable representation. A generalized identity of public interest is insufficient.

### Child-specific process governing implementation of custody

**Governing question:** What process is required before a State abruptly transfers a child from a long-standing recognized placement to a fit parent with whom the child lacks an established relationship.

**Current rule:** When a child has lived for a substantial period in a state-recognized family placement and the State orders an abrupt transfer to a fit parent with whom the child has no established relationship, procedural due process requires notice to the child’s representative and a prompt, focused hearing on immediate safety, timing, continuity, professional support, and transition. The hearing does not authorize a veto over the fit parent’s custody, a new determination of permanent parenthood, or indefinite delay.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court recognizes no general best-interests override of a fit parent, no right to remain permanently with prospective adoptive parents, and no authority for transition planning to become indefinite custody litigation.

**First controlling authority:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Child-specific process governing implementation of custody; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Child-specific process governing implementation of custody; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324: The decision adds a narrow child-specific procedural rule without altering the fit-parent judgment in *Doe*. It does not create a new substantive hierarchy among biological, adoptive, or psychological parents. It governs only the State’s manner of implementing a custody judgment under circumstances presenting a material risk from abrupt disruption.

### Prospective zoning changes applied to existing commercial uses

**Governing question:** Whether applying an adult-business zoning ordinance to businesses operating before enactment violates substantive due process.

**Current rule:** An existing commercial use has no substantive constitutional immunity from an otherwise lawful prospective zoning change. Applying a change to an existing use satisfies substantive due process when the measure bears a substantial relationship to legitimate land-use interests, regulates future location rather than imposing liability for past operation, and provides a substantial transition period together with a nonarbitrary mechanism for hardship relief.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not approve arbitrary confiscation, standardless denial of a hardship extension, selective enforcement, or a pretextual land-use measure.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Substantive due process; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Substantive due process; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

### Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery

**Governing question:** What showing of differential treatment and factual similarity is required to obtain selective-prosecution discovery.

**Current rule:** The showing must address both differential treatment and factual similarity in legally relevant charging characteristics. Statistical concentration may contribute to the showing but does not alone establish entitlement when it fails to identify or reliably describe comparable conduct handled differently. The ultimate burden to prove discriminatory effect and purpose remains on the defendant.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not foreclose discovery on a properly supported claim, require a completed merits case before discovery, or decide the remedy for an ultimately proved constitutional violation.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, May 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 13, 1996, United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456: The decision creates the first controlling simulated selective-prosecution discovery rule. It leaves the ultimate discriminatory-effect, purpose, causation, and persuasion requirements unchanged.

### Gross excessiveness of the remitted award

**Governing question:** Which guideposts determine whether a remitted punitive-damages award remains grossly excessive.

**Current rule:** Constitutional review considers three principal, nonexclusive guideposts: the reprehensibility of the defendant’s conduct; the relationship between punitive damages and actual or potential harm; and the difference between the award and authorized civil or criminal sanctions for comparable misconduct. No fixed numerical ratio governs every case.

**Exceptions and limits:** High ratios may remain permissible when actual damages are difficult to detect, the potential harm is substantially greater than the realized injury, or nominal damages conceal serious misconduct. The guideposts do not establish a mathematical cap.

**First controlling authority:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Gross excessiveness of the remitted award; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Gross excessiveness of the remitted award; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, May 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 20, 1996, BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559: *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* is extended. Meaningful state judicial review remains necessary but is no longer always sufficient: a reviewed award may still violate the substantive constitutional ceiling when it is grossly excessive under the guideposts adopted here. *Honda*’s rejection of a fixed ratio remains unchanged.

### Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order

**Governing question:** What level of proof is required before a State may impose a complete, indefinite, or partly permanent parent-child no-contact order.

**Current rule:** Failure to satisfy the heightened proof requirement bars the long-term complete prohibition. Satisfaction permits the State to impose the protective order otherwise authorized by law. The rule does not govern temporary emergency protection, ordinary visitation scheduling, supervised-contact conditions, or short-term restrictions.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide the constitutionality of the treatment condition; the required form of notice or written findings; emergency procedures; the timing or form of periodic review; or whether and when a separate restoration procedure is required.

**First controlling authority:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, decided November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, decided November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743: The decision establishes a new procedural due-process proof rule for complete, indefinite, or partly permanent parent-child no-contact orders. *Hodge* is distinguished because confidential record retention did not itself alter family relations; Louisiana’s order directly and legally prohibits the relationship.

### Brady materiality of inadmissible information

**Governing question:** When suppressed inadmissible information is material under Brady because it identifies a concrete route to admissible evidence.

**Current rule:** Suppressed inadmissible information may satisfy *Brady* when its disclosed contents would have provided a concrete, record-supported route to admissible evidence concerning a key witness or disputed element. An unspecified hope that further investigation might uncover something useful remains insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not make every undisclosed polygraph result material, permit use of inadmissible results before the jury, or treat speculation about unidentified evidence as sufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, decided October 10, 1995, Brady materiality of inadmissible information; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, decided October 10, 1995, Brady materiality of inadmissible information; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, October 10, 1995.

**Development:**
- October 10, 1995, Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1: *Kyles* is clarified rather than displaced. Its collective-confidence inquiry contains no categorical admissibility prerequisite. The clarification does not eliminate the requirement that the claimed downstream value be concrete and supported by the record.

### Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility

**Governing question:** What knowledge, omission, and causation establish individual supervisory liability for a subordinate’s sexual abuse of students.

**Current rule:** Individual supervisory liability requires proof that the official had actual awareness, established directly or circumstantially, of facts or a pattern plainly pointing to a substantial risk that the subordinate was sexually abusing students; consciously failed to take an obviously necessary protective step within the official’s authority; and thereby caused the plaintiff’s injury.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not constitutionalize negligence, every inadequate investigation, or ordinary school-management error. Municipal liability, the teacher’s separate liability, damages, and the truth of disputed facts remain outside this decision.

**First controlling authority:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, decided October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, decided October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for individual supervisory liability arising from a subordinate public employee’s direct sexual invasion of a student’s bodily integrity. *Elder* and *Johnson v. Jones* are applied without alteration: the complete legal landscape governs clearly established law, while disputed factual sufficiency remains outside interlocutory review.

### Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation

**Governing question:** Whether confidential retention of an accurate record of a ruled-out investigation deprives a family of liberty or property.

**Current rule:** Retention of a confidential record accurately stating that an investigation occurred and was ruled out does not deprive the investigated family of a federal liberty or property interest when the record is not publicly or materially disseminated, does not alter legal status, does not separate the family, and produces no denial of employment, custody, benefit, or other concrete entitlement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern a registry disclosed to employers, licensing bodies, or custody decisionmakers; a record that omits the ruled-out disposition; an entry that automatically imposes a legal disability; or retention contrary to a substantive state-law entitlement to expungement.

**First controlling authority:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, decided October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, decided October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157: *Doe* is distinguished. Its direct constitutional invasion and defendant-specific causal responsibility do not create a general expungement right. The decision establishes a narrow no-deprivation rule for confidential, accurate, nonconsequential retention while reserving materially disseminated or disability-producing registries.

### Indigent access to a first parental-termination appeal

**Governing question:** What access a State must provide when an indigent parent takes a first appeal from a permanent termination decree.

**Current rule:** A State that creates a first appeal from a judgment permanently and exclusively terminating the legal parent-child relationship may not deny meaningful review solely because the parent cannot prepay the record cost. It must furnish a transcript, settled narrative, agreed statement, or another adequate equivalent sufficient for review.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no general right to a civil appeal, appointed appellate counsel, free discovery, or publicly financed litigation concerning money, property, or ordinary custody scheduling.

**First controlling authority:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), decided December 16, 1996, Indigent access to the first termination appeal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), decided December 16, 1996, Indigent access to the first termination appeal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), December 16, 1996.

### Continued federal civil commitment under § 4246

**Governing question:** What current predicates and safeguards permit federal civil commitment to continue beyond the maximum sentence for a dismissed charge.

**Current rule:** Continued custody under § 4246 rests on current mental disease or defect causing current dangerousness, not punishment for the dismissed charge. It requires clear and convincing proof of the current statutory predicate, meaningful periodic review, practical routes to conditional or unconditional release, release when the predicate ends, and renewed reasonable efforts to transfer responsibility to an eligible State. Transfer becomes mandatory when a State accepts responsibility.

**Exceptions and limits:** Incompetence alone, dangerousness without qualifying mental disease, stale findings, nominal review, or retention after lawful state acceptance is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, decided December 16, 1996, Duration and safeguards under § 4246 and state-placement obligation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, decided December 16, 1996, Duration and safeguards under § 4246 and state-placement obligation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, December 16, 1996.

### Incidental employment burdens on the right to marry

**Governing question:** What review governs a public-employer rule that affects spouses’ workplace assignments without prohibiting marriage.

**Current rule:** A governmental anti-nepotism rule that does not prohibit marriage, condition legal recognition, or impose a direct legal obstacle to marrying receives rational-basis review when it causes only incidental employment or economic consequences. It is valid when rationally related to legitimate concerns such as supervision, favoritism, conflicts, and workplace administration.

**Exceptions and limits:** A rule directly conditioning marriage, legal status, or access to a fundamental marital incident may require different review.

**First controlling authority:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, decided March 3, 1997, Anti-nepotism policy and the right to marry; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, decided March 3, 1997, Anti-nepotism policy and the right to marry; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, March 3, 1997.

### Parole-like community release

**Governing question:** When a community-release program creates parole-type procedural protection.

**Current rule:** A release program equivalent to parole in operation creates a protected liberty interest even if the State uses a different label. When the participant lives and works in the community subject to conditions and faces return to prison, revocation requires the core notice, hearing, neutral decisionmaker, disclosure, opportunity to be heard, and written-reason protections associated with parole.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern ordinary institutional classifications or routine changes within prison.

**First controlling authority:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), decided March 18, 1997, Parole-like community release; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), decided March 18, 1997, Parole-like community release; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), March 18, 1997.

### Governmental use of identifiable medical information

**Governing question:** What limits govern governmental acquisition, examination, use, and disclosure of highly intimate identified medical records.

**Current rule:** Identifiable prescription and medical records, particularly records revealing HIV status, receive limited Fourteenth Amendment protection against unjustified governmental acquisition, examination, use, and disclosure. Personally identifying access must remain genuinely and proportionately connected to the actual governmental function. Relevant considerations include sensitivity, whether names rather than aggregate or coded data are necessary, who receives access, the use made of the information, and the safeguards actually maintained.

**Exceptions and limits:** Aggregate or coded auditing, an identified inquiry prompted by concrete fraud or eligibility concerns, legally required disclosure, and confidential retention alone may present different cases. The rule governs governmental, not private-employer, action.

**First controlling authority:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133, decided May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Governmental use of identified medical information; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133, decided May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Governmental use of identified medical information; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133, May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Immediate unpaid suspension after an independent arrest

**Governing question:** When a public employer may impose immediate unpaid suspension without a predeprivation hearing.

**Current rule:** An independent arrest and formal charge supplying reasonable grounds concerning conduct bearing on a sensitive public position may permit immediate temporary unpaid suspension without a prior opportunity to respond, provided the employee receives a constitutionally prompt and meaningful opportunity to contest the continuing consequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize indefinite suspension, reliance on an arrest after contrary information emerges, or immediate action lacking an independent objective basis. A full evidentiary trial is not required before provisional action.

**First controlling authority:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Immediate suspension without pay and prompt post-suspension process; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Immediate suspension without pay and prompt post-suspension process; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), June 9, 1997.

### Fundamental-right identification and physician-assisted suicide

**Governing question:** How an asserted unenumerated liberty is identified and reviewed when the claimed entitlement is physician assistance in suicide.

**Current rule:** The asserted liberty must be identified as the legal entitlement actually sought rather than at the highest level of generality. The court examines constitutional text and structure, precedent, the function of the claimed protection, and relevant legal tradition. Those sources do not establish a fundamental right to obtain intentional physician assistance in suicide, so a prohibition receives rational-basis review.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not permit forced treatment, override competent refusal or advance directives, criminalize good-faith pain relief merely because death is foreseen, or deny lawful palliative care. A materially narrower application involving another independently protected interest remains open.

**First controlling authority:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Asserted fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Asserted fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), June 26, 1997.

### Effective bypass authority in parental-notification laws

**Governing question:** What bypass authority a parental-notification law must provide for a minor seeking an abortion.

**Current rule:** A State may encourage parental consultation and require notice for a minor who cannot establish maturity or a contrary best interest. When notification is otherwise unavoidable, an effective independent decisionmaker must possess authority to authorize the abortion without notice under criteria addressing maturity and best interests. The Constitution does not require one verbal formulation of the best-interests inquiry; it requires genuine authority to dispense with notice when notification is contrary to the minor’s best interests.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decisionmaker need not be a court, and the rule does not prescribe a particular hearing format or decide separate penalty, information, and severability questions.

**First controlling authority:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial review and parental notification; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996, supplies the effective bypass requirement. Lambert v. Wicklund, 520 U.S. 292 (1997), decided March 31, 1997, clarifies that no mandatory phrasing of the best-interests inquiry is required.

**Present operation effective:** Lambert v. Wicklund, 520 U.S. 292 (1997), March 31, 1997.

### Caregiving relationships and the rights of a fit parent

**Governing question:** Whether time in a prospective adoptive placement can displace the unextinguished rights of a fit biological parent.

**Current rule:** Time in a prospective adoptive placement cannot itself transfer permanent parenthood when the adoption lacked the consent or valid termination of a fit parent’s rights. The prospective adoptive parents remain entitled to fair adjudication of their own legal claims, but not to a new comparative custody proceeding that treats possession accumulated during litigation as a constitutional source of superior title.
