**Present operation effective:** Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co., 514 U.S. 476, April 19, 1995.

### Compelled inclusion in a private parade

**Governing question:** Whether a public-accommodations law may compel a private parade organizer to include a message the organizer does not wish to convey.

**Current rule:** Applying a public-accommodations law to compel private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message the organizers do not choose violates the First Amendment. A parade is expressive even when it contains diverse messages, has no single precise theme, and admits participants through a broad selection policy. Selection of participating contingents forms part of the organizers’ expressive composition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not invalidate public-accommodations laws generally, authorize exclusion from ordinary nonexpressive goods or services, or establish that a customer’s identity always constitutes a message.

**First controlling authority:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, decided June 19, 1995, Compelled inclusion in a private parade; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, decided June 19, 1995, Compelled inclusion in a private parade; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, June 19, 1995.

### Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation

**Governing question:** What restrictions government may place on truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation by attorneys.

**Current rule:** Government may not categorically prohibit truthful, nonmisleading targeted direct-mail solicitation for a fixed post-event period merely because recipients or the public may regard prompt solicitation as offensive or undignified. It must identify a substantial interest, establish that the restriction directly and materially advances that interest, and demonstrate a reasonable fit to proven deception, coercion, harassment, or another concrete harm. Least-restrictive-means analysis is not required, but offense, poor taste, and generalized concern for professional image do not substitute for evidence.

**Exceptions and limits:** Supported restrictions on in-person solicitation, deception, coercion, repeated unwanted contact, confidential-record misuse, and proportionate disclosures remain lawful. A materially different evidentiary record may present a different application.

**First controlling authority:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, decided June 21, 1995, Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, decided June 21, 1995, Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, June 21, 1995.

### Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity

**Governing question:** When equal civic access for religious viewpoints becomes unconstitutional direct financing of devotional activity.

**Current rule:** The Free Speech Clause protects religious speakers against exclusion from genuinely opened civic and expressive channels, but it does not require a public university to use mandatory student-fee funds to pay directly for the production of devotional instruction, evangelism, or religious formation. A court must distinguish access to a forum or neutral civic service from transfer of public resources for the designated religious use. Government may not disguise ordinary access as religious funding to justify exclusion. It may, however, decline direct payment for religious exercise while leaving the organization eligible for recognition, facilities, distribution access, and genuinely severable secular benefits.

**Exceptions and limits:** Religious organizations are not categorically ineligible for public programs. They may receive neutral civic services and may compete for genuinely secular benefits on equal terms. The holding concerns the designated religious use of the funds rather than religious identity alone.

**First controlling authority:** Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, 515 U.S. 819, decided June 29, 1995, Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, 515 U.S. 819, decided June 29, 1995, Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, 515 U.S. 819, June 29, 1995.

### Private religious expression in an open government forum

**Governing question:** When private religious expression in an open government forum may be excluded under the Establishment Clause.

**Current rule:** When government genuinely opens a forum on equal terms, it may not exclude otherwise eligible private expression because the viewpoint is religious. The expression remains private when forum history, neutral access rules, private ownership and sponsorship, absence of public financing or editorial control, and available identification measures make private attribution clear. Neutral identification rules may apply, but religious speech may not alone be forced to carry a special disclaimer.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not approve a government-sponsored religious display, selective religious favoritism, captive-audience worship, public financing of the religious message, or circumstances in which the forum’s operation reasonably attributes the expression to the State.

**First controlling authority:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, decided June 29, 1995, Private religious expression in an open public forum; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, decided June 29, 1995, Private religious expression in an open public forum; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, June 29, 1995.

### Attribution of private religious displays inside government buildings

**Governing question:** When a private religious display inside a government building is attributable to the government.

**Current rule:** Neutral access for a clearly identified private religious display does not violate the Establishment Clause merely because the forum is inside a government building. Attribution depends on the forum’s established character, neutral access rules, private ownership and sponsorship, governmental control or financing, location, and contextual measures making private authorship clear.

**Exceptions and limits:** A display selected, financed, designed, controlled, or presented by the State as its own may be attributed to government. The Court also reserves a pretextual forum, obscured sponsorship, or circumstances in which the State affirmatively adopts the message.

**First controlling authority:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, decided June 29, 1995, Establishment Clause attribution inside the Capitol; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, decided June 29, 1995, Establishment Clause attribution inside the Capitol; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, June 29, 1995.

### Attribution and the Establishment Clause

**Governing question:** What facts determine whether private religious activity in a public-school access program is attributable to the school.

**Current rule:** In a public-school access program, neutral access for private religious activity is not attributable to the school when the meetings are voluntary, privately run, outside instructional time, dependent on parental consent, unsupported by public financing, accompanied by a clear private-sponsorship disclaimer, and neither selected nor led by school personnel. Attribution remains a contextual inquiry into sponsorship, control, timing, funding, consent, and the presentation of the activity to students and parents.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not require access during instruction, authorize teacher-led recruitment or devotion, excuse disruption or coercion, or bar neutral rules governing supervision and safety. It also does not require the District to retain the forum indefinitely if it closes or redefines it through genuinely viewpoint-neutral rules.

**First controlling authority:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Attribution and the Establishment Clause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Attribution and the Establishment Clause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501: *Rosenberger* is distinguished because no public money is transferred for devotional activity. *Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch* are applied to an after-school facility program, with parental consent, private sponsorship, timing, public control, and disclaimers supplying the attribution inquiry. The decision adds a recurring school-access application without changing those rules.

### Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program

**Governing question:** Whether a school may exclude religious viewpoints from an after-school access program open to comparable secular activity.

**Current rule:** Once a school opens a private-access channel for a defined subject, it may impose reasonable subject-matter and operational restrictions, but it may not identify a religious perspective on the permitted subject as the forbidden viewpoint.

**First controlling authority:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”

**Governing question:** Whether a voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God” remains a civic observance rather than a devotional exercise.

**Current rule:** A school-led civic observance may include a brief, historically embedded acknowledgment of religion when its object and operation remain patriotic rather than devotional and students retain a complete, practical right of nonparticipation. The government may not use that principle to conduct prayer, religious instruction, profession of faith, or an exercise that penalizes or formally identifies dissenters.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not permit punishment, compelled standing, compelled departure, teacher-led prayer, devotional elaboration on the Pledge, or selective treatment of students who decline to join. It does not govern a materially different exercise whose language or administration converts civic acknowledgment into religious profession.

**First controlling authority:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, decided February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, decided February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437: *Good News/Good Sports Club* is distinguished. Its protected expression was privately sponsored and admitted through a neutral access program; the Pledge is government selected and government led. The present decision creates a narrow rule for a voluntary, nondevotional civic observance and does not expand the private-forum attribution rules.

### Facial First Amendment challenge

**Governing question:** What showing permits facial invalidation of an access-protection statute that also reaches plainly regulable obstruction.

**Current rule:** Peaceful advocacy, prayer, leafletting, picketing, persuasion, and association remain protected. The access-related intent element identifies the conduct Congress regulated; it does not make opposition to abortion an element of liability. Difficult boundary applications must be addressed on their facts rather than through facial invalidation of the statute’s plainly legitimate sweep.

**Exceptions and limits:** A prosecution resting on ambiguous rhetoric, passive presence, immaterial delay, protected counseling, or viewpoint-selective enforcement remains open to as-applied review. RFRA and other issues outside the controlled grant are not decided.

**First controlling authority:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial First Amendment challenge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial First Amendment challenge; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider

**Governing question:** What First Amendment framework applies when government ends a regular service-provider relationship for political affiliation or speech.

**Current rule:** A claim based principally on compelled political allegiance or affiliation is governed by *Elrod* and *Branti*. A claim based on independent speech is governed by the *Pickering* balance adapted to the contractual setting. In either event, *Mt. Healthy* preserves a defense that the same action would have occurred for lawful reasons.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern first-time applicants or competitive bidders, decide whether political affiliation was appropriate for any particular service, or determine qualified immunity, damages, reinstatement, or the mayor’s final policymaking authority.

**First controlling authority:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, decided June 28, 1996, Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, decided June 28, 1996, Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, June 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712: The decision extends *Elrod*, *Branti*, and *Pickering* beyond formal public employment to an established service-provider relationship. Together with *Umbehr*, it distinguishes constitutionally forbidden retaliation from the absence of a property entitlement and from lawful contracting discretion.

### Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses

**Governing question:** How courts evaluate alternative avenues when adult-business zoning applies to businesses already operating at enactment.

**Current rule:** A municipality need not guarantee an adult business its current address, preferred neighborhood, least expensive parcel, or commercially optimal site. It must leave a meaningful field of lawful locations in practical operation. Relocation expense and economic conditions matter when they demonstrate that nominally available sites are functionally unavailable, but ordinary commercial inconvenience does not establish a First Amendment violation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court establishes no universally sufficient acreage, number of sites, or amortization period. A site count fails when the listed parcels are unavailable in practice.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

**Development:**
- March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858: *City of Renton* and *City of Ladue* are clarified for alternative avenues. Practical availability, rather than a nominal parcel count, controls; relocation cost matters when it demonstrates functional unavailability, but the Court creates no fixed acreage, site-count, or amortization safe harbor.

### Official sectarian symbols used as governmental identity

**Governing question:** When a religious symbol incorporated into a government seal violates the Establishment Clause.

**Current rule:** Government violates the Establishment Clause when it selects an unmistakably sectarian symbol and pervasively uses it as the government’s own official identifying mark, unless the complete design, context, history, and official use establish a genuinely secular governmental meaning rather than religious endorsement. Private-expression attribution rules do not apply to a symbol chosen and used by government as its own.

**Exceptions and limits:** Historical significance, longevity, and secular images are relevant but not independently controlling. The rule does not govern private religious expression in a neutral forum.

**First controlling authority:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, decided March 31, 1997, Christian cross in the official city seal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, decided March 31, 1997, Christian cross in the official city seal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, March 31, 1997.

### Private religious viewpoints in limited public access programs

**Governing question:** When government may exclude private religious teaching or worship from a program opened to comparable private subjects.

**Current rule:** When government opens a limited access program to private educational, cultural, philosophical, or religious subjects, it may define the permitted subject matter through reasonable, viewpoint-neutral rules. It may not exclude an otherwise eligible presentation because it affirmatively teaches, advocates, or practices a religious viewpoint on a permitted subject. Avoiding an Establishment Clause violation does not justify exclusion when neutral access, private sponsorship, and attribution safeguards keep the expression private.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government need not sponsor worship, finance devotional materials, allow disruption, or admit expression outside the defined program. It may enforce neutral rules against obstruction, coercion, and interference.

**First controlling authority:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, decided May 27, 1997, Religious viewpoint exclusion from the senior-center program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, decided May 27, 1997, Religious viewpoint exclusion from the senior-center program; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, May 27, 1997.

### Public-employee religious expression and government attribution

**Governing question:** What distinctions govern a public employer’s regulation of an employee’s private religious expression at work.

**Current rule:** A public employer may regulate use of subordinate labor, official resources, coercion, harassment, and expression reasonably attributable to government. It may not impose a religion-specific ban on voluntary private prayer, witnessing, counseling, or religious items merely because coworkers object, when comparable private expression is allowed and the conduct does not use public authority or resources. Any asserted Establishment Clause justification must be objectively grounded in attribution or coercion rather than offense alone.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect official worship, compelled participation, use of subordinates or government resources for personal religious activity, harassment, or disruption of public operations.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Religion-specific directives and attribution; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Religion-specific directives and attribution; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, March 17, 1997.

### Content-based criminal regulation of Internet indecency

**Governing question:** What scrutiny governs content-based criminal restrictions on protected indecent speech over a general-purpose communications network.

**Current rule:** A content-based criminal restriction on Internet expression must define prohibited content with adequate precision, satisfy strict scrutiny, and preserve realistic adult access. Severe penalties, uncertain content categories, nationwide distribution, community-standard uncertainty, and speaker-side access controls that exclude lawful adults all enter facial vagueness, overbreadth, and tailoring review. Scienter concerning transmission does not cure uncertainty about what speech the law makes criminal.

**Burdens and proof:** Government bears the burden of establishing narrow tailoring and the inadequacy of practical, materially less speech-restrictive means of limiting minors’ access.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect obscenity, child sexual-abuse material, true threats, fraud, unlawful solicitation, or direct harmful contact with minors, and it does not classify every Internet service identically.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Content-based criminal suppression of protected Internet speech; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Content-based criminal suppression of protected Internet speech; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), June 26, 1997.

### RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable

**Governing question:** What substantive standard governs a RFRA claim when a valid source of federal authority makes the statute applicable.

**Current rule:** The claimant must establish a substantial burden on religious exercise. Government then bears production and persuasion that application of the particular burden furthers a compelling governmental interest through the least restrictive means. Review is provision specific and application specific. This substantive formulation operates only when RFRA validly applies under a constitutional source of federal authority.

**Burdens and proof:** Claimant bears the substantial-burden showing; government bears production and persuasion on compelling interest and least restrictive means.

**Exceptions and limits:** City of Boerne forecloses universal application to state and local government under Fourteenth Amendment § 5. RFRA’s application to the Federal Government and other asserted sources was not decided in OT1996.

**First controlling authority:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, RFRA substantial-burden and strict-scrutiny formulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, supplies the provision-specific formulation. Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274, decided March 27, 1995, supplies the third-party-protection application. City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, preserves those mechanics only where a valid source makes RFRA applicable.

**Present operation effective:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), June 25, 1997.

**Development:**
- June 25, 1997, City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997): The substantive RFRA mechanics survive only for applications resting on a valid source of federal authority; universal state and local application under § 5 ceased.

### Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality

**Governing question:** When an adult-business zoning ordinance qualifies for content-neutral review under the secondary-effects doctrine.

**Current rule:** An adult-business zoning ordinance receives content-neutral treatment only when its enacted design and actual justification are genuinely directed at adverse secondary effects. Courts consider the text, enactment record, evidence before the decisionmakers, alternatives selected or rejected, and practical operation. Evidence that suppression of protected expression materially controlled the governmental decision may defeat secondary-effects classification. An improper statement by one participant does not establish that conclusion by itself.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government may not convert censorship into a content-neutral regulation by reciting secondary effects, commissioning post hoc studies, or disguising a practical prohibition as zoning. A materially different record showing pretext, discriminatory administration, deliberate expressive suppression, or a practical ban remains subject to content-based review.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

## Fourth Amendment

### Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives

**Governing question:** Whether otherwise reasonable deadly force becomes excessive because an officer might have used a less-deadly alternative.

**Current rule:** The Fourth Amendment does not require an officer to exhaust every feasible less-deadly tactic before using otherwise reasonable deadly force. Earlier police conduct may bear on the totality when officers unreasonably create or materially intensify the need for force. Reasonableness is assessed without hindsight from the circumstances confronting the officer. Availability of another tactic is relevant evidence only insofar as it bears on whether the force actually used was objectively unreasonable. It does not create a least-deadly-means requirement or authorize courts to direct tactical sequencing after the event.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not protect an officer who needlessly manufactures a confrontation, uses force after the threat has ended, or relies on earlier unlawful conduct that proximately creates the asserted necessity.

**First controlling authority:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, January 9, 1995.

### Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record

**Governing question:** When the exclusionary rule applies to an officer’s objectively reasonable reliance on an erroneous court-maintained warrant record.

**Current rule:** The exclusionary rule does not require suppression when an officer makes an arrest in objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained record that incorrectly reports an outstanding warrant, where the record identifies an isolated court-clerical failure and shows no police participation or recurring systemic unreliability. The court considers whether exclusion could materially deter the governmental actors responsible for the error and whether the officer executing the apparent warrant acted objectively reasonably. The holding does not create a general exception for inaccurate databases.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide errors generated or maintained by police, recurring inaccuracies known to law enforcement, reckless recordkeeping, or a system whose unreliability makes officer reliance objectively unreasonable.

**First controlling authority:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, March 1, 1995.

### Technological acquisition of information from inside a home

**Governing question:** When technological acquisition of information from inside a home constitutes a Fourth Amendment search.

**Current rule:** Law-enforcement use of sense-enhancing technology from outside a home to obtain information about activity within the home that ordinary public observation could not otherwise acquire constitutes a Fourth Amendment search. The thermal scan of Pinson’s residence was a search. Physical entry is unnecessary when officers deliberately use technological means to obtain otherwise unavailable information concerning activity behind the walls of a home. The constitutional inquiry does not depend on whether the instrument detects light, sound, heat, or another physical signal.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary visual observation from a lawful vantage remains unaffected. The decision does not address smoke, odor, or other conditions perceptible without specialized technology; utility records obtained through lawful process; every technological measurement near private property; or surveillance that reveals no information concerning activity inside a home. The Court expresses no view on the remaining affidavit, probable cause, suppression, or *United States v. Leon*.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056, decided May 8, 1995, Technological acquisition of information from inside a home; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056, decided May 8, 1995, Technological acquisition of information from inside a home; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056, May 8, 1995.

### Knock and announce as a component of reasonableness

**Governing question:** When officers executing a warrant may dispense with ordinary announcement.

**Current rule:** Knock and announce is a component of Fourth Amendment reasonableness. Officers may enter without prior announcement when particular facts create reasonable suspicion that announcement would be dangerous or futile, permit escape, or materially inhibit the investigation through destruction of evidence. Reasonable suspicion is less demanding than probable cause but must arise from the circumstances of the actual execution; the charged offense or disposable character of evidence alone does not create a categorical exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** A magistrate’s advance ruling does not prevent officers from responding to later exigent facts, and advance authorization does not eliminate review of the execution actually undertaken. No fixed waiting period is established.

**First controlling authority:** Wilson v. Arkansas, 514 U.S. 927, decided May 22, 1995, Knock and announce as part of reasonableness; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wilson v. Arkansas, 514 U.S. 927, decided May 22, 1995, establishes the constitutional component. Richards v. Wisconsin, 520 U.S. 385 (1997), decided April 28, 1997, supplies the reasonable-suspicion standard and rejects offense-based categorical exceptions.

**Present operation effective:** Richards v. Wisconsin, 520 U.S. 385 (1997), April 28, 1997.

**Development:**
- April 28, 1997, Richards v. Wisconsin, 520 U.S. 385 (1997): The exceptions identified in Wilson now require case-specific reasonable suspicion; no drug-offense category eliminates knock and announce.

### Privacy in a rental vehicle

**Governing question:** When a driver who is not listed on a rental agreement has a reasonable expectation of privacy in the vehicle.

**Current rule:** A driver’s absence from a rental agreement does not by itself defeat a legitimate expectation of privacy when the driver possesses and controls the vehicle with the authorized renter’s permission and without theft, fraud, or comparable wrongful possession. The inquiry considers the defendant’s actual relationship to the vehicle, including permission, possession of the keys, practical control, ability to exclude strangers, the owner’s retained rights, and whether possession was obtained or continued wrongfully. A private contractual driver restriction is relevant but not conclusive.

**Exceptions and limits:** Permission from the renter does not automatically establish privacy under every circumstance. The Court does not protect a thief, carjacker, fraudulent renter, or person continuing possession after an effective repossession.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Privacy in a rental vehicle; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Privacy in a rental vehicle; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, May 30, 1995.

### Independent privacy in closed luggage

**Governing question:** Whether a person retains an independent expectation of privacy in closed luggage placed inside another person’s vehicle.

**Current rule:** A person’s lack of a legitimate expectation of privacy in a vehicle does not by itself eliminate a legitimate expectation of privacy in that person’s closed luggage found within the vehicle. Privacy in a container depends on ownership, possession, control, and steps taken to preserve its contents as private. The Government may nevertheless search the container when the automobile exception, consent, a valid inventory, or another lawful authority reaches it.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide ownership of each bag, disclaimer, abandonment, the scope of probable cause, or the validity of any inventory procedure.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Independent privacy in closed luggage; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Independent privacy in closed luggage; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, May 30, 1995.

### Suspicionless drug testing of public-school athletes

**Governing question:** When a public school may require suspicionless drug testing of student athletes.

**Current rule:** A public school may conduct suspicionless drug testing of interscholastic athletes when the students’ reduced privacy expectations in the athletic program, the minimally intrusive collection and restricted use of results, and a documented drug problem together make the search reasonable. Participation does not create a general waiver of Fourth Amendment protection.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision concerns a non-law-enforcement program limited to interscholastic athletes and restricted drug information. It does not approve disclosure to police, testing for unrelated medical conditions, or suspicionless testing of every student regardless of context.

**First controlling authority:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, decided June 26, 1995, Random urinalysis of interscholastic athletes; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, decided June 26, 1995, Random urinalysis of interscholastic athletes; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, June 26, 1995.

### Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop

**Governing question:** Whether a traffic stop supported by objective probable cause is invalid because of an officer’s subjective motive.

**Current rule:** The constitutional inquiry ordinarily asks whether the facts objectively authorized the seizure actually performed. A court does not suppress evidence because an officer also possessed an unexpressed investigative motive or because another officer might have exercised enforcement discretion differently.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize a stop lacking objective legal support, fabricated violations, prolongation beyond the traffic mission without independent justification, or discriminatory enforcement. A claim that officers selected motorists because of race remains governed by equal protection and its own proof and remedial rules.

**First controlling authority:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, decided June 10, 1996, Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, decided June 10, 1996, Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806: *Ornelas* is applied without alteration. That decision governs appellate review of the objective constitutional determination; *Whren* establishes that subjective enforcement purpose ordinarily does not enter the Fourth Amendment merits. *Armstrong* remains a separate equal-protection and discovery rule.

### Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause

**Governing question:** What standard of appellate review applies to determinations of reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

**Current rule:** The trial court determines what occurred, resolves credibility, and identifies the immediate factual setting. The appellate court independently applies the constitutional standard to those facts. Practical consideration of local knowledge and police experience does not transfer final authority over the constitutional boundary.

**Exceptions and limits:** Independent review does not authorize appellate factfinding, disregard of supported credibility determinations, or use of a mechanical checklist of suspicious circumstances. The Court does not decide whether suppression is ultimately required.

**First controlling authority:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, decided May 28, 1996, Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, decided May 28, 1996, Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, May 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 28, 1996, Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690: *Thompson v. Keohane* is applied and clarified. Its distinction between historical facts and independent application of an objective constitutional rule now governs reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

### Interception through another participant’s vulnerable endpoint

**Governing question:** Whether a caller loses Fourth Amendment protection because the other participant unknowingly uses a vulnerable communications device.

**Current rule:** A person using a protected landline or cellular telephone does not lose Fourth Amendment protection merely because the other participant, without the caller’s knowledge, uses a vulnerable cordless handset. Participant consent, a willing informant, knowing exposure, or acceptance of the retransmission presents a different case. Technical accessibility alone does not establish voluntary exposure of the unaware caller’s own words.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not determine the other participant’s privacy interest or disturb participant-consent cases.

**First controlling authority:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Fourth Amendment protection of the noncordless caller; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Fourth Amendment protection of the noncordless caller; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, December 9, 1996.

### Passenger-exit orders during lawful traffic stops

**Governing question:** Whether an officer may order a passenger out of a vehicle during a lawful traffic stop.

**Current rule:** During a lawful traffic stop, an officer may categorically order passengers to exit the vehicle while the stop remains in progress. The initial stop must itself be objectively valid, and the authority controls passenger location only for the duration and purposes of that stop.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize prolongation of the stop, frisk without the required suspicion, or unrelated detention after the traffic mission ends.

**First controlling authority:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Passenger-exit authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Passenger-exit authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), February 19, 1997.

### Suspicionless testing requires a concrete special need

**Governing question:** What threshold government must satisfy before imposing suspicionless drug testing outside ordinary law enforcement.

**Current rule:** Reduced test intrusiveness does not itself justify departure from individualized suspicion. Government must identify a substantial and concrete special need connected to the actual duties or risks of the covered class, beyond ordinary law enforcement or symbolic reassurance, and then establish context-specific privacy, efficacy, and fit. A blanket test for candidates for public office fails when no real operational risk is shown.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern individualized suspicion, medical fitness examinations, private testing, financial disclosure, or security screening tied to a real and immediate safety risk.

**First controlling authority:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), decided April 15, 1997, Suspicionless testing of candidates; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), decided April 15, 1997, Suspicionless testing of candidates; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), April 15, 1997.

### Stored pager data and contemporaneous preservation search

**Governing question:** What Fourth Amendment rules govern activation and immediate retrieval of a pager’s stored messages after arrest.

**Current rule:** Activating a pager to display concealed numerical messages is a search distinct from physical seizure. Officers may contemporaneously retrieve the short numerical memory of a pager seized from an arrestee at the place of arrest when retrieval addresses the ordinary risk that incoming pages will overwrite the finite stored sequence. The authority is confined to immediate, limited display serving that preservation function.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize delayed exploration after the preservation justification ends or examination of materially broader, durable, or unrelated data stores.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, decided May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Activation of stored pager memory and immediate retrieval incident to arrest; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, decided May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Activation of stored pager memory and immediate retrieval incident to arrest; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

## Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity

### Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity

**Governing question:** When a bistate compact entity qualifies as an arm of a State for Eleventh Amendment immunity.

**Current rule:** An entity created under the Compact Clause is not presumed to share the compacting States’ Eleventh Amendment immunity unless the compact and implementing law supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their own alter ego and that Congress concurred. State-treasury responsibility is highly probative, but not categorically dispositive.

**Exceptions and limits:** A materially different compact that makes an entity a direct state alter ego, subjects state funds to its judgments, or otherwise identifies its obligations as state obligations may produce a different result.

**First controlling authority:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, decided November 14, 1994, Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, decided November 14, 1994, Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, November 14, 1994.

### Article I abrogation of state sovereign immunity under IGRA

**Governing question:** When Congress may authorize a prospective federal action against a State as an incident of an Article I commerce power.

**Current rule:** Under controlling *Union Gas*, Congress may abrogate state sovereign immunity when it unmistakably identifies the State as defendant, acts within an enumerated commerce power, and expressly supplies the cause of action and prospective remedy. A statute authorizing a tribe to sue a State for failure to negotiate in good faith under the Indian Commerce Clause satisfies those conditions when Congress supplies that clear action and a bounded prospective remedial sequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** Article I does not itself create a cause of action, and statutory clarity remains indispensable. The rule does not authorize damages, an implied cause of action, a judicially drafted compact, or abrogation outside an enumerated congressional power. Whether an officer action is independently available when the statute does not authorize suit against the State remains governed by separate authority.

**First controlling authority:** Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, decided March 27, 1996, Article I abrogation under IGRA; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, decided March 27, 1996, Article I abrogation under IGRA; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, March 27, 1996.

### Prospective officer relief for ongoing federal tribal property violations

**Governing question:** Whether the Eleventh Amendment bars prospective relief against state officials when resolving the federal claim requires deciding disputed title.

**Current rule:** The Eleventh Amendment does not bar prospective declaratory and injunctive relief against responsible state officials to end an alleged ongoing violation of federal tribal property rights, even when the court must resolve a disputed title question to determine whether future official conduct is unlawful. The complaint must identify officials with a genuine enforcement connection and seek relief directed to future conduct.

**Exceptions and limits:** The officer action may not award damages, retrospective compensation, an accounting payable by the State, execution against state property, or a decree requiring the State itself to convey title.

**First controlling authority:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Prospective enforcement of federal tribal property rights; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Prospective enforcement of federal tribal property rights; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), June 23, 1997.

### Anti-commandeering of state executive officers

**Governing question:** When Congress may require state or local executive officers to administer a federal regulatory program.

**Current rule:** Outside a constitutionally assigned source-specific exception, Congress may not command state or local executive officers, as officers of their States, to administer or enforce a federal regulatory program. Congress may regulate private actors, preempt state law, create federal offices, enact generally applicable rules reaching state employees as persons, contract with willing States, obtain voluntary assistance, and attach lawful funding conditions. It may not transfer federal administration to unwilling state executives by direct command.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not affect state-court obligations under the Supremacy Clause, valid preemption, voluntary agreements, information sharing, lawful spending conditions, or duties expressly authorized by another constitutional provision.

**First controlling authority:** Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997), decided June 27, 1997, Mandatory state-executive administration; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997), decided June 27, 1997, Mandatory state-executive administration; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997), June 27, 1997.

## Federalism and Preemption

### State consumer-fraud regulation

**Governing question:** When the Airline Deregulation Act preempts application of a state consumer-fraud law to airline rates, routes, or services.

**Current rule:** The ADA preempts application of the Illinois Consumer Fraud Act to frequent-flyer restrictions directly related to airline rates and services. A State may not impose prescriptive standards governing airline rates, routes, or services through consumer-protection law. A generally applicable statute is preempted when its particular application directs or polices the carrier’s primary economic conduct in those fields.

**First controlling authority:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, State consumer-fraud regulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, State consumer-fraud regulation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, January 18, 1995.

### Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations

**Governing question:** When the Airline Deregulation Act permits state-law enforcement of an airline’s self-imposed contractual obligations.

**Current rule:** The ADA does not preempt routine enforcement of an airline’s own contractual commitments. A court may determine and enforce the parties’ agreement through ordinary contract law and ordinary contract remedies. It may not enlarge, reform, or supplement the undertaking through state policies external to the bargain.

**Exceptions and limits:** Good faith, implied terms, and related doctrines may be used to identify and enforce the actual agreement, but not to recreate consumer regulation under a contract label.

**First controlling authority:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, January 18, 1995.

### Express preemption without a federal standard in force

**Governing question:** Whether an express preemption clause displaces state duties when no federal standard addressing the product or conduct is in force.

**Current rule:** The Safety Act’s express preemption clause does not displace state requirements concerning a vehicle-safety feature when no federal standard governing the same aspect of performance is in effect.

**First controlling authority:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Express preemption without a federal standard in force; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Express preemption without a federal standard in force; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, April 18, 1995.

### Implied conflict preemption

**Governing question:** When state law is impliedly preempted because compliance conflicts with an operative federal requirement or objective.

**Current rule:** The state defect claims are not impliedly preempted because compliance with both state and federal law is possible and the claims obstruct no identified federal objective. Agency inaction does not establish a federal policy requiring freedom from state law unless the statute, an operative regulation, or an adequately demonstrated federal decision shows that nonregulation itself was the chosen means of accomplishing federal objectives.

**Exceptions and limits:** A valid federal standard governing the same performance feature, or an affirmative and lawfully adopted federal policy requiring a uniform no-regulation result, could present a different preemption question.

**First controlling authority:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Implied conflict preemption; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Implied conflict preemption; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, April 18, 1995.

### Common-law requirements and parallel duties

**Governing question:** When common-law product duties are preempted as requirements and when equivalent parallel duties survive.

**Current rule:** The remedial form of a state duty does not determine preemption. The court must compare the conduct required by state law with the binding federal counterpart. A genuinely equivalent state duty is not different from or additional to federal law merely because state law supplies damages.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not create a private federal cause of action, decide tort liability, or determine preemption for a device subjected to full premarket approval or another specific federal condition.

**First controlling authority:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, Common-law requirements and parallel duties; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, Common-law requirements and parallel duties; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, June 26, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 26, 1996, Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470: *Myrick* is clarified for an express clause keyed to requirements applicable to a product. The decision adds claim-specific comparison, confirms that common-law duties may count as requirements, and establishes that equivalent parallel duties survive. *Myrick*’s rejection of preemption by regulatory absence or generalized federal policy remains unchanged.

### General manufacturing and labeling rules

**Governing question:** How courts determine whether general federal manufacturing or labeling rules preempt a particular state-law claim.

**Current rule:** Express preemption requires a binding federal requirement applicable to the particular device or the relevant aspect of its performance, a state requirement concerning the same subject, and a material difference or addition between them. Regulatory generality, market permission, or agency inaction does not by itself establish that federal law selected an exclusive device standard.

**First controlling authority:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, General manufacturing and labeling rules; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, General manufacturing and labeling rules; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, June 26, 1996.

## Commerce Clause

### Origin-based charges and compensatory-burden defense

**Governing question:** When a state charge triggered by out-of-state origin survives dormant Commerce Clause review as compensatory.

**Current rule:** A charge triggered expressly by interstate origin is presumptively invalid. A compensatory defense requires identification of an actual intrastate tax or burden imposed on a substantially equivalent in-state event for the same governmental purpose; generalized estimates of additional public costs do not substitute for that corresponding burden.

**Burdens and proof:** The State bears the demanding burden of justifying the facial discrimination and establishing the substantially equivalent intrastate burden.

**Exceptions and limits:** The State may impose uniform charges, regulate genuine health or environmental risks evenhandedly, and recover demonstrable costs through nondiscriminatory measures.

**First controlling authority:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), decided April 4, 1994, Origin-based waste surcharge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), decided April 4, 1994, Origin-based waste surcharge; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), April 4, 1994.

### Operational discrimination favoring a local private enterprise

**Governing question:** When a formally local routing requirement discriminates against interstate commerce by reserving a market for a favored private enterprise.

**Current rule:** A law is discriminatory when its practical operation directs commerce to a favored local private enterprise, even if disfavored local firms are excluded alongside out-of-state firms. Such a law is invalid unless the government establishes a legitimate local objective that cannot adequately be served through reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives.

**Burdens and proof:** The government bears the burden of showing both the legitimate local objective and the inadequacy of reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not condemn evenhanded health and safety regulation or decide a materially different arrangement involving a genuinely public facility and distinct governmental-market circumstances.

**First controlling authority:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Exclusive local processing requirement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Exclusive local processing requirement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), May 16, 1994.

### Linked market assessment and local-producer subsidy

**Governing question:** When a facially evenhanded market assessment becomes discriminatory because of a formally linked local-only distribution.

**Current rule:** A State discriminates against interstate commerce when it links an otherwise evenhanded market assessment to distribution of the entire resulting fund exclusively to in-state producers competing in the assessed market, so that the integrated program shifts the economic burden away from local producers and onto their interstate competitors. Formally linked components are evaluated as one economic arrangement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not invalidate an evenhanded tax merely because revenue enters the general treasury, prohibit local subsidies financed from general revenues, or constitutionalize ordinary public-spending choices.

**First controlling authority:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Combined assessment and in-state producer subsidy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Combined assessment and in-state producer subsidy; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), June 17, 1994.

### Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket

**Governing question:** Whether a State may impose a nondiscriminatory sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket sold within the State.

**Current rule:** A conventional sales tax may be measured by the full price of an interstate transportation service when the taxable event is a discrete local sale, the tax is internally consistent, the State’s claim to the sale is externally consistent, interstate and intrastate transactions receive equal treatment, and the tax is fairly related to state-provided services. Apportionment is not required merely because performance of the purchased service later crosses state lines.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize multiple taxation of the same sale, discriminatory origin-based taxes, or unapportioned taxes on receipts or income earned through multistate operations. A tax failing internal or external consistency remains invalid.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, decided April 3, 1995, Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, decided April 3, 1995, Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, April 3, 1995.

### Federal regulation of noneconomic local possession

**Governing question:** When Congress may regulate noneconomic local possession under the Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** The Commerce Clause does not authorize a stand-alone federal prohibition of noneconomic local firearm possession near schools based only on an attenuated chain from violence, to education, to productivity, and finally to the national economy. Congress may regulate the channels of interstate commerce; instrumentalities, persons, and things in interstate commerce, including intrastate threats to them; and economic activity whose properly defined class substantially affects an actual interstate market. Congress may also reach local conduct whose inclusion is reasonably necessary to a comprehensive and otherwise valid regulation of interstate economic activity. It may not aggregate noneconomic local conduct through a chain of generalized social and economic consequences that would erase the distinction between enumerated commerce authority and a general police power.

**Exceptions and limits:** The judgment creates no constitutional right to possess a firearm near a school. States retain general police authority over weapons, schools, and local crime. Congress remains free to regulate interstate firearm commerce, protect channels and instrumentalities, enact an offense with a constitutionally meaningful interstate element, or include local conduct within a comprehensive valid economic regime.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Lopez, 514 U.S. 549, decided April 26, 1995, Federal regulation of noneconomic local possession; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Lopez, 514 U.S. 549, decided April 26, 1995, Federal regulation of noneconomic local possession; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Lopez, 514 U.S. 549, April 26, 1995.

### Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality

**Governing question:** Whether Congress may directly protect an interstate transportation instrumentality from local obstruction or damage.

**Current rule:** The Commerce Clause permits Congress to protect a motor vehicle against a direct intrastate forcible taking when the federal offense requires proof of the particular vehicle’s interstate connection. An offense-specific commerce element must perform real constitutional work rather than add a remote interstate fact to an ordinary local crime. A direct attack on possession and control of a transportation instrumentality, combined with proof that the particular vehicle moved through interstate commerce, falls within Congress’s authority over instrumentalities and things in commerce.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not hold that every local offense involving an object that once crossed a state line is federal, that every crime occurring in or near a car is subject to federal jurisdiction, or that generalized insurance and productivity effects are sufficient. The holding rests on the direct forcible seizure of the transportation instrumentality and the statute’s vehicle-specific interstate requirement.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, April 27, 1995.

### Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction

**Governing question:** When Congress may regulate conduct directed at obstructing access to services in an interstate market under the Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Congress may regulate access-directed conduct whose object and operation are to prevent transactions and services in an interstate market. The constitutional connection must arise from the regulated conduct’s direct interference with the market itself, not from a generalized chain through social costs, health consequences, productivity, or crime.

**Exceptions and limits:** FACE’s access-directed conduct and intent elements must perform real work in every application. The decision does not authorize federal prosecution of every local assault, trespass, protest, or disturbance associated with a medical facility.

**First controlling authority:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642: *Lopez* is clarified rather than narrowed. Generalized economic consequences remain insufficient, but conduct directed at preventing provision and receipt of services in an actual interstate market may be regulated because the interference operates on the market itself.

### Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility

**Governing question:** When a municipality’s criminally enforceable routing of waste to a private facility violates the dormant Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Governmental financial exposure, planning responsibility, or contractual access to a facility does not convert a coercive legal command into a market purchase. A routing rule backed by fines or imprisonment and applicable to persons outside governmental contractual privity remains regulation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not govern a genuinely public facility operating under distinct public-service circumstances, an evenhanded health regulation, or a municipality’s own purchase of disposal capacity.

**First controlling authority:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Routing within a municipally purchased service

**Governing question:** When a municipality acts as a market participant in routing waste within a service it purchases, and when contractual conditions exceed that purchased transaction.

**Current rule:** A government buyer may define the suppliers, route, facility, and performance it will purchase within the transaction. Market-participant protection ends when a contractual condition controls independent transactions, noncontracting actors, commercial waste outside the purchased service, or downstream commerce in which government is not participating.

**First controlling authority:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Routing within a municipally purchased service; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Routing within a municipally purchased service; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502: Simulated *Carbone* is applied and clarified. Its private-enterprise discrimination rule governs Smithtown’s ordinance, while its reservation of genuinely public and governmental-market arrangements remains intact. The decision adds a recurring boundary between contractual participation in a purchased service and coercive regulation of commerce outside contractual privity.

### Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market

**Governing question:** When Congress may include local possession within regulation of a defined interstate product market.

**Current rule:** Congress may include local possession within a valid regulation of a defined interstate product market when possession and transfer are functionally inseparable and excluding possession would permit concealment, retention, or acquisition that defeats the market prohibition. Generalized effects of violent crime, insurance costs, fear, or lost productivity remain insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not establish a general federal police power over firearms, validate every possession statute, or hold that any object remains federally regulable forever merely because it once crossed a state line. The anti-evasion relationship to a direct market regime must perform real work.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, decided June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, decided June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016: *Lopez* is clarified. Its rejection of attenuated aggregate-effects reasoning remains intact, while a direct product-market prohibition may include possession that is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion of the regulated transfer market.

### Interstate computer-assisted procurement transactions

**Governing question:** When Congress may regulate a commercial transaction completed through an interstate computer transmission.

**Current rule:** Congress regulates interstate commerce itself when it prohibits a knowing interstate computer transmission made as an operative part of a commercial transaction to procure an interest in another State’s lottery. Aggregate-effects analysis is unnecessary when the regulated conduct is the cross-border transaction or transmission itself. The offense-specific interstate element must perform genuine work.

**Exceptions and limits:** A nominal jurisdictional element, generalized moral concern, or purely local gambling is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate lottery procurement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate lottery procurement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Congressional authorization of state restraints on interstate commerce

**Governing question:** When Congress may permit a state restriction otherwise barred only by the dormant Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Congress may unmistakably authorize state restrictions that would otherwise violate the dormant Commerce Clause. The authorization must be clear in the federal enactment and may alter only dormant-commerce limitations, not an independent constitutional prohibition.

**Exceptions and limits:** Authorization does not validate a materially different state restriction outside the federal structure or permit violation of another constitutional guarantee.

**First controlling authority:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate-agreement exception; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate-agreement exception; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Residency-based charitable tax exemptions

**Governing question:** When a charitable property-tax exemption discriminates against interstate commerce.

**Current rule:** A State facially discriminates against interstate commerce when it denies a charitable tax exemption because an institution principally serves nonresidents while granting the exemption to comparable charities serving residents.

**Exceptions and limits:** States need not subsidize charitable activity everywhere and may use neutral criteria based on nonprofit status, property use, finances, or regulatory compliance. Direct resident public benefits present a different question.

**First controlling authority:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997), decided May 19, 1997, Residency-based charitable tax treatment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997), decided May 19, 1997, Residency-based charitable tax treatment; supplies the current rule.
