Courts may consider objective unreasonableness, frivolousness, motivation, compensation, deterrence, and other circumstances faithful to copyright’s purposes, applying all considerations evenhandedly.

**Exceptions and limits:** The considerations are nonexclusive, no single one is mandatory, and § 505 does not establish automatic prevailing-party fee shifting.

**First controlling authority:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), decided March 1, 1994, Evenhanded discretionary awards under § 505; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), decided March 1, 1994, Evenhanded discretionary awards under § 505; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), March 1, 1994.

## Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act

### No economic-motive element in RICO

**Governing question:** Whether RICO requires the enterprise or alleged predicate acts to pursue profit or another economic objective.

**Current rule:** RICO contains no economic-motive element. Ideological purpose neither establishes nor defeats liability; a civil plaintiff must prove the enacted enterprise, pattern, qualifying predicates, injury to business or property, and required causal connection.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide whether particular conduct satisfies the predicates or pattern, and protected advocacy, association, picketing, and persuasion do not become racketeering merely because they accompany a political movement.

**First controlling authority:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Economic motive under RICO; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Economic motive under RICO; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), January 24, 1994.

### A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations

**Governing question:** What direct interstate operations make a RICO enterprise engaged in interstate commerce under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a).

**Current rule:** An enterprise is engaged in interstate commerce under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a) when its own continuing operations include interstate purchasing, interstate recruitment or movement of labor, interstate shipment of output, or comparable direct interstate activity. When that direct commerce is proved, no separate substantial-effects analysis is necessary.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether a purely intrastate enterprise with only remote economic consequences satisfies § 1962(a).

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, decided May 1, 1995, A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, decided May 1, 1995, A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, May 1, 1995.

### Separate accrual without revival of stale civil RICO injuries

**Governing question:** How later predicate acts affect limitations for earlier and later civil RICO injuries.

**Current rule:** A later predicate act does not revive damages for an older time-barred injury. A later act causing a genuinely new and independent injury may support a separately accruing claim limited to that injury and resulting damages. Each injury must be connected to the act causing it; a timely new injury does not reset the period for completed older harm merely because both acts are part of one alleged pattern.

**Exceptions and limits:** The universal civil RICO accrual trigger remains unresolved.

**First controlling authority:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Separate accrual and no revival of stale injuries; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Separate accrual and no revival of stale injuries; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), June 19, 1997.

### Fraudulent-concealment diligence in civil RICO

**Governing question:** What a civil RICO plaintiff must prove to toll limitations through fraudulent concealment.

**Current rule:** A plaintiff relying on fraudulent concealment must prove concealment and reasonable diligence in attempting to discover the cause of action. Concealment does not toll the period when reasonable investigation would have disclosed the claim.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant bears persuasion on concealment and reasonable diligence under the governing equitable doctrine.

**Exceptions and limits:** No universal form of investigation is prescribed, and the rule does not decide concealment that could not be overcome through reasonable diligence.

**First controlling authority:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Fraudulent concealment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Fraudulent concealment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), June 19, 1997.

## Taxation

### Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities

**Governing question:** Whether § 3124(a) exempts income from repurchase agreements collateralized by federal securities.

**Current rule:** Section 3124(a) does not exempt the income because the mutual funds receive interest on private loans to seller-borrowers, not interest on obligations of the United States. Federal securities used as collateral do not transform the return on an independent private lending obligation into federally exempt interest. The transaction is classified according to its legal and economic operation rather than the parties’ sale-and-repurchase labels.

**Exceptions and limits:** A transaction conveying genuine beneficial ownership of federal securities and the accompanying federal coupon or discount income may present a different question.

**First controlling authority:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, decided December 12, 1994, Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, decided December 12, 1994, Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, December 12, 1994.

### ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2)

**Governing question:** Whether ADEA backpay and liquidated damages are excluded from gross income under § 104(a)(2).

**Current rule:** ADEA backpay and liquidated damages are not excluded from gross income under § 104(a)(2). Exclusion requires both a cause of action based on tort or tort-type rights and damages received on account of personal injuries or sickness. Each component of a recovery must satisfy the statutory causal requirement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide damages expressly compensating physical injury, medical injury, or an emotional injury under a statute and record satisfying the enacted exclusion.

**First controlling authority:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, decided June 14, 1995, ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, decided June 14, 1995, ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, June 14, 1995.

### Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit

**Governing question:** Whether § 4371 may tax premiums for insurance covering goods in export transit under the Export Clause.

**Current rule:** Section 4371 may not be applied to premiums paid for insurance covering goods in export transit because that application imposes a tax within the Export Clause’s categorical prohibition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not exempt exporters from income, property, payroll, or other generally applicable taxes imposed on independent events unrelated to export transit. The Court does not decide every possible tax on a service connected more remotely to exports.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, decided June 10, 1996, Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, decided June 10, 1996, Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843: The decision creates the first controlling simulated Export Clause rule. A tax inseparably incident to the export transit of goods remains forbidden even when generally applicable and nondiscriminatory; taxes on legally independent events remain outside the holding.

## Federal Indian Law

### Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax

**Governing question:** How courts determine the legal incidence of a state motor-fuels tax and when a tax on a Tribe or member in Indian country is valid.

**Current rule:** When the legal incidence of a state tax falls directly on a Tribe or tribal member inside Indian country, the tax is invalid absent clear congressional authorization. Courts determine legal incidence from the enacted liability, payment, reporting, and pass-through provisions rather than from economic burden alone.

**Exceptions and limits:** Oklahoma may prospectively redesign the tax within federal limits. The Court does not decide a tax whose incidence is lawfully placed on a nontribal distributor or consumer.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, June 14, 1995.

### Income earned from the Tribe

**Governing question:** When a State may tax wages paid by a Tribe to a tribal member based on the member’s residence.

**Current rule:** A State may not tax wages paid by a Tribe to a tribal member residing in Indian country. It may tax the wages of a member residing outside Indian country absent federal law providing an exemption. Reservation residence and tribal-source income trigger the territorial protection; tribal employment alone does not carry that protection to an off-reservation residence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern nonmember employees, income from nontribal sources, or an express federal alteration of state taxing authority.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Income earned from the Tribe; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Income earned from the Tribe; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, June 14, 1995.

## Environmental Law

### Scope of Clean Water Act § 401 certification conditions

**Governing question:** What project conditions a State may impose once an activity that may result in a discharge triggers Clean Water Act § 401 certification.

**Current rule:** A potential discharge determines whether certification is required. Once triggered, § 401(d) authorizes conditions on the applicant or licensed activity that are necessary to assure compliance with applicable federal and state water-quality requirements, including adopted designated uses supported by the administrative record.

**Exceptions and limits:** Certification is not a general license to regulate conduct unrelated to water quality or the licensed activity. A flow condition must remain tied to an adopted designated use or other applicable water-quality requirement and have record support.

**First controlling authority:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Scope of conditions under § 401(d); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Scope of conditions under § 401(d); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), May 31, 1994.

### Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife

**Governing question:** When habitat modification constitutes a harm take under the ESA and what injury and causation the Government must prove.

**Current rule:** The ESA permits the Secretary to define “harm” as significant habitat modification or degradation that actually kills or injures protected wildlife, including injury caused by material impairment of essential behavioral patterns. Habitat alteration alone is insufficient. The Government must establish actual death or injury to protected wildlife and the ordinary causal connection between the modification and that injury. Speculative, conjectural, or merely potential ecological effects do not constitute a completed take.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not reach habitat modification causing no actual injury, eliminate ordinary causation, or authorize regulation outside the ESA’s protected-species and permit structure.

**First controlling authority:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, decided June 29, 1995, Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, decided June 29, 1995, Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, June 29, 1995.

## Administrative Law

### Delegated power to modify does not authorize elimination

**Governing question:** How far an agency may alter a statutory requirement under a delegated power to modify it.

**Current rule:** A delegated power to modify permits bounded alteration, including adjustments to form, content, timing, and genuinely limited exceptions. It does not authorize an agency to eliminate a central statutory requirement for nearly an entire regulated sector or replace the enacted regime with a fundamentally different model. Deference does not extend authority beyond a meaning the statutory text can bear.

**Exceptions and limits:** The agency may make incremental adjustments and grant genuinely limited waivers consistent with the statutory delegation.

**First controlling authority:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Scope of the power to modify tariff-filing requirements; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Scope of the power to modify tariff-filing requirements; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), June 17, 1994.

### Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233

**Governing question:** Whether Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233 is an interpretive rule exempt from notice and comment or creates a new binding obligation.

**Current rule:** Section 233 is an interpretive rule that explains the application of existing reasonable-cost principles to advance refunding and did not require notice-and-comment rulemaking. An agency guideline is interpretive when it explains how existing statutory and regulatory standards apply to a recurring transaction without creating a new binding obligation inconsistent with those standards. A manual may not be used to amend the governing law or impose an obligation outside the statute and regulations.

**First controlling authority:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, March 6, 1995.

### Deference to authoritative interpretations of genuinely ambiguous regulations

**Governing question:** What weight a court gives an agency’s interpretation of its own regulation.

**Current rule:** After using ordinary interpretive tools, if an agency regulation remains genuinely ambiguous, the agency’s authoritative interpretation controls unless plainly erroneous or inconsistent with the regulation, provided it reflects fair and considered judgment rather than a convenient post hoc rationalization. An interpretation stated in an amicus brief may qualify when it expresses the agency’s established authoritative position and is not manufactured for the litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Deference cannot make regulatory text bear an unreasonable meaning and does not apply to an inconsistent, secret, plainly erroneous, or merely convenient interpretation.

**First controlling authority:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Interpretation of an agency’s own regulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Interpretation of an agency’s own regulation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), February 19, 1997.

## Statutory Interpretation

### No implied claim-ending sanction for missed statutory timing directives

**Governing question:** When a court may attach dismissal or loss of governmental authority to violation of a statutory timing command.

**Current rule:** Mandatory timing language imposes duties on responsible officials, but a court does not create a claim-ending dismissal sanction or loss of governmental authority that Congress omitted, particularly when Congress separately enacted an express limitations period. The absence of that sanction does not make the timing directives optional or foreclose independently preserved constitutional objections to delay.

**Exceptions and limits:** A different result follows when Congress expressly supplies dismissal, loss of authority, or another consequence.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Effect of the statutory timing directives; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Effect of the statutory timing directives; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), December 13, 1993.

### Presumption against retroactive legal consequences

**Governing question:** How a court determines the temporal reach of a statute enacted while a case is pending.

**Current rule:** A court first asks whether Congress clearly prescribed temporal reach. If not, it determines whether application would attach new legal consequences to completed conduct, including increased liability, impairment of an existing right, imposition of a new duty, or creation or revival of a private action previously unavailable. A jurisdictional label does not avoid retroactivity analysis when the amendment changes whether a private cause of action exists rather than merely reallocating an existing action among tribunals. Statutory structure, including an express pending-case instruction in one companion chapter and its omission from another, may supply the temporal answer.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Identify a controlling temporal command first. If none exists, determine retroactive legal effect. Apply a more specific enacted structural answer when one is available.

**Exceptions and limits:** Genuinely procedural or forum-allocating provisions may apply to pending matters when they do not alter substantive exposure or completed legal consequences. Congress may prescribe retroactive operation clearly.

**First controlling authority:** Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, Temporal reach of newly enacted remedies; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, supplies the general sequence. Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., 511 U.S. 298 (1994), applies it to expanded conduct coverage. Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer, 520 U.S. 939 (1997), decided June 16, 1997, supplies the private-action and jurisdictional-label boundary. Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, supplies the companion-chapter structural inference.

**Present operation effective:** Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997), June 23, 1997.

**Development:**
- June 16, 1997, Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer, 520 U.S. 939 (1997): A jurisdictional label does not remove an amendment from retroactivity analysis when it creates or revives a private action.
- June 23, 1997, Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997): An express pending-case instruction in one simultaneously enacted chapter and its omission from another may establish different temporal operation.

## Remedies

### Section 1983 remedy for state interference with a concrete federal labor right

**Governing question:** When state interference with a federal labor right is actionable under 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

**Current rule:** Section 1983 is available against an appropriate state official when federal labor law protects a concrete individual interest against state abridgment, the governmental duty is judicially administrable, and Congress has not displaced the remedy with an exclusive alternative enforcement scheme. Federal preemption by itself does not automatically create an individual § 1983 cause of action.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not waive state sovereign immunity, authorize damages against the State, or predetermine the proper defendant and form of relief.

**First controlling authority:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 1983 remedy for state interference with the federal labor right; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 1983 remedy for state interference with the federal labor right; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Municipal liability

**Governing question:** Whether a municipality can be liable on a policy claim when the underlying shooting is constitutionally reasonable and no distinct municipal injury exists.

**Current rule:** A municipal policy claim cannot rest solely on conduct that caused no constitutional injury. When the underlying use of force was constitutionally reasonable and no distinct constitutional injury is attributable to municipal policy, the municipal claim fails.

**First controlling authority:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Municipal liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Municipal liability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, January 9, 1995.

### Relief limited to represented public-employee plaintiffs

**Governing question:** How far relief may extend beyond a represented class of public employees to nonparty officials.

**Current rule:** Relief may protect the represented class, but it may not extend to senior Executive Branch officials who are not parties or represented members.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Scope of the injunction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Scope of the injunction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, February 22, 1995.

### Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule

**Governing question:** Whether reliance on an invalidated state statute permits a state court to withhold a controlling federal constitutional rule from a nonfinal case.

**Current rule:** A state court may not deny the ordinary retroactive application of a controlling federal constitutional rule to a nonfinal case merely because a party relied on the invalidated state statute. Once this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs other cases still open on direct review. A different outcome remains possible when an independently valid legal doctrine supplies it, such as an alternative cure, a preexisting remedial rule, qualified immunity, res judicata, or a doctrine limiting retroactivity itself. Simple reliance on the former law does not create a free-standing exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary rules concerning final judgments, statutes of repose, waiver, harmless error, qualified immunity, and other independently valid remedial doctrines remain intact.

**First controlling authority:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, decided May 15, 1995, Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, decided May 15, 1995, Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, May 15, 1995.

### Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility

**Governing question:** Whether the unmistakability, sovereign-acts, reserved-powers, or impossibility doctrines bar contract damages after a regulatory change.

**Current rule:** When authorized federal contracts allocate the financial risk that later law will prevent promised regulatory treatment, the later law remains fully effective but does not extinguish the Government’s obligation to pay damages for breach. Because damages preserve sovereign authority while enforcing the financial consequence of the bargain, the unmistakability, sovereign-acts, reserved-powers, and impossibility doctrines do not bar recovery merely because lawful sovereign action prevented performance.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Government does not insure against every later legal change, and agencies may not bargain away constitutional or nondelegable sovereign powers. Respondents remain subject to ordinary contract limitations on causation, foreseeability, mitigation, and proof of loss.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, July 1, 1996.

### Effect of an express severability command

**Governing question:** When a federal court must honor an express state severability command.

**Current rule:** A federal court must give effect to an express state severability command when the surviving text can function independently and severance neither rewrites the provision nor creates a statutory scheme the legislature did not enact. Judicial speculation about the legislature’s preferred comprehensive bargain cannot override its enacted instruction.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court expresses no view on § 302(3)’s emergency provisions, gestational operation, undue-burden consequences, facial validity, or enforceability in any particular application.

**First controlling authority:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, decided June 17, 1996, Effect of an express severability command; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, decided June 17, 1996, Effect of an express severability command; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, June 17, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 17, 1996, Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for an express state severability command. The abortion standards established in simulated *Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer* and applied earlier this term in *Planned Parenthood v. Miller* remain unchanged and govern only the constitutional review reserved for remand.

### Federal immunity from compensatory damages

**Governing question:** What statutory language is required to waive the United States’ immunity from compensatory damages.

**Current rule:** A substantive statutory command applicable to a federal agency does not authorize a money judgment against the United States unless enacted text expresses that consent unambiguously. Remedial cross-references or plausible structural inferences cannot supply a waiver when the statutory language supports competing readings.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not diminish § 504’s substantive prohibition, disturb Lane’s reinstatement, foreclose prospective relief, or determine damages against a nonfederal recipient under a separately authorized action.

**First controlling authority:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, decided June 20, 1996, Federal immunity from compensatory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, decided June 20, 1996, Federal immunity from compensatory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, June 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 20, 1996, Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187: *FDIC v. Meyer* is applied and clarified. Waiver of immunity does not follow from the existence of a right or an available cause of action, and a right applicable to the Federal Government does not itself authorize payment from the Treasury.

### State substantive limit on compensatory damages

**Governing question:** When a federal diversity court must apply a substantive state limit on compensatory damages and what mechanism federal law controls.

**Current rule:** A state rule that materially narrows the damages available on a state-created claim must be given effect in diversity when disregarding it would alter outcomes substantially and encourage forum selection. Federal law continues to govern the judicial mechanism and allocation through which the state limit is applied.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not import New York’s full appellate structure, determine the proper damages figure, or establish a federal numerical cap.

**First controlling authority:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, State substantive limit on compensatory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, State substantive limit on compensatory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, June 24, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 24, 1996, Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415: Simulated *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* remains unchanged. It concerns constitutionally adequate review of punitive damages and supplies no Erie or Seventh Amendment allocation rule for state compensatory damages. *Gasperini* creates the first controlling simulated rules for applying a state damages-tolerance standard in federal diversity court.

### Representative standing for statutory damages

**Governing question:** What Article III and germaneness showings permit an association to seek statutory damages for its members.

**Current rule:** Article III requires at least one represented member to possess injury, causation, and redressability. Germaneness assures a genuine representative stake. Once those requirements are met, Congress may authorize an association to litigate common liability and seek member relief even though individual participation is needed to establish the amount due.

**Exceptions and limits:** The association may not recover for persons who are not members or injured, eliminate individual defenses, or dispense with proof of each claimant’s compensable loss.

**First controlling authority:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, decided May 13, 1996, Representative standing for statutory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, decided May 13, 1996, Representative standing for statutory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, May 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 13, 1996, United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544: The decision clarifies *Hunt*. Member standing remains constitutional; the individual-participation prong is an administrability rule subject to congressional alteration. The Court creates no general rule permitting representative damages suits without statutory authorization.

### Territorial limits on punitive authority

**Governing question:** What territorial conduct a State may punish through punitive damages without imposing its policy in other States.

**Current rule:** A State may punish conduct occurring within its lawful regulatory interest, but it may not use punitive damages to impose its policy throughout the Nation or punish transactions lawful in other States as independent offenses.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not prevent consideration of other conduct when independently relevant under lawful evidentiary rules, but the State may not punish the defendant for lawful out-of-state transactions themselves.

**First controlling authority:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Territorial limits on punitive authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Territorial limits on punitive authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, May 20, 1996.

### Identification of statutory rights enforceable through § 1983

**Governing question:** What a claimant must identify before invoking § 1983 to enforce a federal spending-program statute.

**Current rule:** The claimant must identify a particular statutory provision intended to benefit the claimant, imposing a binding obligation, and defining an interest sufficiently concrete for judicial enforcement. The inquiry proceeds provision by provision. Aggregate program standards and an entire statutory title cannot be treated as one indivisible entitlement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not foreclose enforcement of a specific rights-creating provision or itself establish a structural remedy.

**First controlling authority:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Identification of enforceable Title IV-D rights; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Identification of enforceable Title IV-D rights; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), April 21, 1997.

### Congressional displacement of § 1983

**Governing question:** When a federal statute displaces the presumptive § 1983 remedy for a concrete federal right.

**Current rule:** After a concrete federal right is identified, § 1983 remains presumptively available unless Congress expressly foreclosed it or created a remedial scheme so comprehensive and incompatible that individual enforcement cannot coexist with the statutory design. General audits, corrective action, and funding reductions do not categorically displace every individual action.

**Exceptions and limits:** A specific remedial provision may displace § 1983 for a particular claim. The rule creates no substantive right by itself.

**First controlling authority:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Statutory displacement of § 1983; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Statutory displacement of § 1983; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), April 21, 1997.

### Consent-decree termination and Rule 52 findings

**Governing question:** What findings a court must make before dissolving an institutional consent decree under a substantial-compliance condition.

**Current rule:** Substantial compliance does not require perfection or literal satisfaction of every numerical criterion. It requires performance equivalent to the decree’s essential purposes with only immaterial deviations. Before dissolution, the court must identify the governing obligations, explain whether departures materially impair the decree’s objectives, state how quantitative and qualitative evidence was evaluated, and make subsidiary findings sufficient for meaningful appellate review under Rule 52(a).

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not expand the decree beyond its terms, require perfect compliance, or permit continued federal control after the written termination condition is established.

**First controlling authority:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, decided April 21, 1997, Substantial compliance and Rule 52 findings; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, decided April 21, 1997, Substantial compliance and Rule 52 findings; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, April 21, 1997.

### Single final-policymaker hiring liability

**Governing question:** When a municipality may be liable under § 1983 for a final policymaker’s single hiring decision.

**Current rule:** A single hire may constitute municipal policy, but attribution alone does not establish fault or causation. Liability requires an underlying constitutional violation, final policymaking authority in the relevant field, conscious disregard of a substantial and highly predictable applicant-specific risk that conferring the particular authority will produce the kind of injury that occurs, and a direct causal connection. Negligent screening, favoritism, general unsuitability, or unrelated misconduct is insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not constitutionalize state hiring qualifications, impose respondeat superior, or decide training, supervision, or retention after later notice.

**First controlling authority:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), decided April 28, 1997, Municipal liability for a final policymaker’s single hire; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), decided April 28, 1997, Municipal liability for a final policymaker’s single hire; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), April 28, 1997.

### Rule 60(b)(5) and still-controlling precedent

**Governing question:** When doctrinal erosion permits relief from a prospective injunction under Rule 60(b)(5).

**Current rule:** Doctrinal tension or erosion does not by itself make continued enforcement inequitable when the injunction rests on a Supreme Court precedent that has not been overruled. A lower court must apply the still-controlling precedent. Rule 60(b)(5) relief requires an actual change in controlling law, a material change in facts, or another legally sufficient basis making continued prospective enforcement inequitable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prevent relief after controlling law actually changes or material factual conditions change. It does not authorize reopening completed liability merely because later decisions cast doubt on the reasoning of still-controlling precedent.

**First controlling authority:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Rule 60(b)(5) relief while controlling precedent remains intact; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Rule 60(b)(5) relief while controlling precedent remains intact; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), June 23, 1997.

### Qualified immunity for for-profit private prison employees

**Governing question:** Whether guards employed by a competitive, for-profit prison-management company receive qualified immunity.

**Current rule:** Employees of a private, for-profit prison-management company are not entitled to qualified immunity when the firm systematically performs custody for profit in a competitive market, uses ordinary insurance and employment arrangements, and is not integrated into the State’s civil-service, indemnification, or closely supervised public-officer structure. State action remains distinct from immunity.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern a person briefly assisting government, a nonprofit or uniquely public institution, a contractor under direct day-to-day governmental control, or a separate good-faith defense.

**First controlling authority:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Qualified immunity for guards employed by a for-profit prison firm; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Qualified immunity for guards employed by a for-profit prison firm; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), June 23, 1997.

### Scope and design of institutional access-to-courts relief

**Governing question:** How institutional relief must be fitted to proven denial of prisoner access to the courts.

**Current rule:** Structural relief must identify the prisoners, institutions, and legal-access channels affected; cure the proven barrier effectively; omit program details unnecessary to that cure; and leave operational choice to prison officials when multiple lawful solutions exist. Systemwide relief remains available when systemwide or structurally recurring injury is actually proved.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar language assistance, trained help, monitoring, institution-wide measures, or retained jurisdiction when those devices are necessary to correct a demonstrated barrier.

**First controlling authority:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Scope and design of institutional relief; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Scope and design of institutional relief; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, June 24, 1996.

### Desegregation expenditures and race-determinative scholarships

**Governing question:** When a federal desegregation decree may compel institutional expenditures or race-determinative scholarships.

**Current rule:** A federal desegregation court may compel expenditures necessary to eliminate an identified surviving vestige, but may not order general institutional equalization, prestige enhancement, or demographic attraction without the required violation-remedy connection. A race-determinative scholarship independently receives strict scrutiny and must serve a compelling remedial interest while remaining narrowly tailored in necessity, amount, duration, termination, and treatment of workable race-neutral alternatives.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State may voluntarily improve institutions or use race-neutral recruitment and scholarships beyond the federal constitutional minimum. The rule concerns what a federal decree may compel.

**First controlling authority:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Funding, facilities, endowments, and scholarships; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Funding, facilities, endowments, and scholarships; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Monitoring and incremental withdrawal of institutional control

**Governing question:** How long monitoring and retained jurisdiction may continue in an institutional desegregation decree.

**Current rule:** Monitoring, retained jurisdiction, administrative funding, and a general injunction may operate only in relation to surviving traceable vestiges and valid remedial measures. Supervision must terminate incrementally when the constitutional work in a separable component is complete.

**Exceptions and limits:** No fixed termination date is required. Different components may remain under supervision for different periods.

**First controlling authority:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Monitoring, injunctions, and withdrawal of control; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Monitoring, injunctions, and withdrawal of control; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

## Admiralty and Maritime Law

### State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death

**Governing question:** When state remedies supplement or are displaced for a nonseafarer’s death in territorial waters.

**Current rule:** A court must distinguish the general maritime cause of action from status-specific federal statutes, location-specific federal statutes, and supplementary state remedies. Federal displacement follows from an applicable exclusive statute or controlling maritime rule, not from an abstract preference for uniformity.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide remedies for seamen, longshore workers, deaths governed by the Death on the High Seas Act, or a state rule that directly conflicts with a specific federal maritime command.

**First controlling authority:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, January 9, 1996.

### Nominal LHWCA compensation for future wage loss

**Governing question:** When a permanent work injury with no present wage loss supports nominal compensation.

**Current rule:** The LHWCA permits nominal compensation when a permanent injury causes no present loss of wage-earning capacity but the claimant proves a significant, nonconjectural potential that the injury will reduce future capacity. The employer seeking termination of actual benefits first bears the burden to prove current earnings fairly represent present capacity; the claimant seeking nominal compensation then bears persuasion by a preponderance on significant future potential.

**Exceptions and limits:** Permanent impairment alone, an abstract possibility of unemployment, or administrative convenience is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Nominal compensation and future earning capacity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Nominal compensation and future earning capacity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), June 19, 1997.

## Federal Contracts

### Contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk

**Governing question:** When authorized federal officials form a binding contract allocating regulatory-change risk and what remedy follows later sovereign action.

**Current rule:** The United States is bound when authorized officials, acting within statutory contracting and assistance powers, make an express, bargained-for commitment that reasonably allocates a defined regulatory risk. The promise need not disable future legislation; it may instead require compensation if lawful sovereign action prevents performance.

**Exceptions and limits:** Not every forbearance letter, regulatory statement, or agency prediction is contractual. Authority cannot be created by promise, and the result depends on authorized, transaction-specific commitments supported by consideration.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, July 1, 1996.

**Development:**
- July 1, 1996, United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for governmental contracts allocating the financial consequences of later legislation. Congress’s power to change substantive law remains intact; an authorized contract may nonetheless require damages when the Government accepted consideration and assumed the relevant risk.

## Fourteenth Amendment Enforcement

### Claim-specific enforcement under Fourteenth Amendment § 5

**Governing question:** When congressional legislation is valid Fourteenth Amendment § 5 enforcement as applied to a claim.

**Current rule:** A court first determines whether the plaintiff states the statutory claim on the conduct alleged and whether that same conduct independently violates a Fourteenth Amendment guarantee as this Court has construed it. If the conduct independently violates the guarantee, the statute is valid enforcement as applied to that conduct, the remedy Congress provided attaches to that claim, and the § 5 inquiry ends. If the conduct does not independently violate the guarantee, the statute must satisfy four requirements drawn from its enacted terms and the governing constitutional standard.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** First, the operative command must run against a State, its subdivisions, or persons acting under state authority. Second, the statute must be referable to a specific Fourteenth Amendment guarantee, using this Court’s standard for state action in that field as the baseline. Third, a statute reaching only conduct that baseline forbids is valid without more. A statute that also reaches state conduct the Constitution permits is valid only if it allocates proof under the constitutional standard, supplies remedial or compliance machinery attached to that standard, or imposes a broader prohibition while either preserving a defense of constitutional compliance on the same evidence that would defeat a constitutional claim or using an enacted coverage criterion with a stated reauthorization interval or a bailout keyed to present conditions. Fourth, Congress may not supply the rule of decision for whether the Constitution has been violated, direct the standard of review courts apply to a constitutional claim, or legislate so that the constitutional standard governs for no purpose. When the constitutional violation includes a mental-state or remedial-inadequacy element, the plaintiff must plead and prove that element to proceed on the independently unconstitutional conduct.

**Exceptions and limits:** The volume of congressional findings neither satisfies nor defeats any requirement. Validity is assessed claim by claim and remedy by remedy, and failure as to one application does not invalidate the statute or provision as to others. Validity is measured against the constitutional standard at enactment; a later narrowing operates prospectively. The rule does not decide RFRA’s validity as applied to the Federal Government, authority under another enumerated power or a lawful spending condition, whether § 5 reaches private conduct, abrogation of state sovereign immunity, or broader state-law protection of religious exercise.

**First controlling authority:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Section 5 enforcement power; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Section 5 enforcement power; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), June 25, 1997.

## Patent Law

### Allocation of patent-claim construction

**Governing question:** Whether the court or jury construes a patent claim, including technical terms of art within the claim.

**Current rule:** Construction of a patent claim, including the meaning of technical terms of art within the claim, is a question for the court. The jury retains its role in deciding infringement and other factual issues under the claim as construed. Expert testimony may inform the court’s interpretation of the patent instrument, but the presence of technical evidence does not transfer ultimate claim construction to the jury.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not remove infringement from the jury, classify every expert-supported dispute as legal, or decide the appellate standard for every possible subsidiary factual determination.

**First controlling authority:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, decided April 23, 1996, Allocation of patent-claim construction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, decided April 23, 1996, Allocation of patent-claim construction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, April 23, 1996.

### Doctrine of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel

**Governing question:** How infringement by equivalents operates by claim element and how unexplained claim amendments affect the inquiry.

**Current rule:** The doctrine of equivalents remains available but operates element by element and may not erase a claim limitation. Courts determine claim meaning and the legal effect of prosecution history; genuine factual equivalence remains for ordinary infringement procedures. A claim amendment made for a substantial reason related to patentability may create estoppel. When the record does not disclose the reason, the patentee bears the burden of establishing a reason that does not surrender the asserted equivalent; failure produces a presumption that the equivalent was relinquished.

**Exceptions and limits:** The doctrine cannot expand a claim beyond its element-specific boundaries or nullify a surrendered limitation.

**First controlling authority:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), decided March 3, 1997, Survival and element-specific operation of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), decided March 3, 1997, Survival and element-specific operation of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), March 3, 1997.

## Civil Procedure

### Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement

**Governing question:** When a court may use inherent authority to bar a fugitive from defending a related civil action.

**Current rule:** An exercise of inherent judicial authority must be a reasonable and proportionate response to an actual problem affecting the proceeding in which the sanction is imposed. Complete loss of the right to defend is unavailable when narrower measures can protect enforcement, evidence, discovery, and the integrity of related litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect disobedience of a court order, require unrestricted civil discovery, decide ownership or traceability, or resolve whether Congress may enact a constitutionally adequate rule addressing a specifically defined form of fugitive abuse.

**First controlling authority:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, decided June 10, 1996, Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, decided June 10, 1996, Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, June 10, 1996.

### Federal trial and appellate allocation for state excessiveness review

**Governing question:** How federal trial and appellate courts divide review under a state-law compensatory-damages excessiveness standard.

**Current rule:** The federal district judge applies the state excessiveness standard through the historically grounded Rule 59 new-trial power. Appellate review is limited to abuse of discretion.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar appellate correction of legal error or abuse of discretion and does not govern constitutional review of punitive damages.

**First controlling authority:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, Federal trial and appellate allocation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, Federal trial and appellate allocation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, June 24, 1996.

### Settlement-only class certification under Rule 23

**Governing question:** What Rule 23 requires when certification is sought only to implement a settlement.

**Current rule:** A settlement-only class must satisfy every applicable Rule 23 requirement. Settlement may eliminate trial-management difficulties, but it does not relax commonality, typicality, predominance, or adequate representation. Predominance fails when individual and state-law questions overwhelm common issues, and one undivided representation structure is inadequate when currently injured and future claimants have materially conflicting interests.

**Exceptions and limits:** Mass-tort settlement classes are not categorically impossible. Narrower claim groupings, separately represented subclasses, and individual settlements remain available.

**First controlling authority:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 23 in a settlement-only class and adequate representation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 23 in a settlement-only class and adequate representation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), June 25, 1997.

## Rules no longer current: First Amendment

### RFRA review of prospective state and local enforcement

**Former governing question:** Whether RFRA could govern prospective state and local enforcement under Fourteenth Amendment § 5.

**Last operative rule:** RFRA required provision-specific substantial-burden, compelling-interest, and least-restrictive-means review of state and local enforcement.

**Ceased to govern:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, held that RFRA’s universal application to state and local government exceeded Congress’s § 5 power.

**Successor:** RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable, under First Amendment.

**Surviving force:** The substantive strict-scrutiny formulation survives for any RFRA application resting on a valid source of federal authority. The final judgment in Church of Scientology remains intact.

**Controlling authorities:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514; City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997).

### RFRA exemptions removing third-party antidiscrimination protection

**Former governing question:** Whether RFRA required a state or local commercial-housing exemption that would remove another person’s statutory protection.

**Last operative rule:** When RFRA was treated as applicable to state and local government, government could deny an exemption that would remove an identified applicant’s antidiscrimination protection when no less restrictive accommodation preserved that protection.

**Ceased to govern:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, displaced RFRA’s state and local application under § 5.

**Successor:** RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable, under First Amendment.

**Surviving force:** The third-party-protection analysis remains relevant only in a RFRA application supported by a valid federal source. The Free Exercise rule for neutral and generally applicable housing laws remains current.

**Controlling authorities:** Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274; City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997).
